Finite-Graph-Cover-Based
Analysis of Factor Graphs in Classical and Quantum Information Processing Systems

Abstract




\keepXColumns

Abstract of thesis entitled: Finite-Graph-Cover-Based

Analysis of Factor Graphs in Classical and Quantum Information Processing Systems

Submitted by HUANG, Yuwen

for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy

at The Chinese University of Hong Kong in September 2024

Various intractable computational problems can be reduced to computing the marginals and the partition function of a suitably defined multivariate function represented by a factor graph.
For computational problems relating to classical statistical models, we consider standard factor graphs (S-FGs),
i.e.
, factor graphs with local functions taking on non-negative real values. For computational problems relating to quantum information processing systems, we consider so-called double-edge factor graphs (DE-FGs), a class of factor graphs where local functions take on complex values and satisfy some positive semi-definiteness constraints. The sum-product algorithm (SPA) provides an efficient iterative method for approximating the marginals and the partition function induced by each of these factor graphs.

For an arbitrary S-FG, Yedidia
et al.
showed that fixed points of the SPA on this S-FG correspond to stationary points of the Bethe free energy function. Based on this, they defined the so-called Bethe approximation of the partition function, or simply the Bethe partition function, in terms of the minimum of the Bethe free energy function. Later, Vontobel provided a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of the finite graph covers of the S-FG. The proof of this characterization heavily relied on the method of types.

In this thesis, we leverage finite graph covers to analyze the SPA and the Bethe partition function for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. There are two main contributions in this thesis.

The first main contribution concerns a special class of S-FGs where the partition function of each S-FG equals the permanent of a nonnegative square matrix. The Bethe partition function for such an S-FG is called the Bethe permanent. A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe permanent is given by the degree-
M
Bethe permanent, which is defined based on the degree-
M
graph covers of the underlying S-FG.
In this thesis, we prove a degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based lower bound on the
permanent of a non-negative square matrix, resolving a conjecture proposed by
Vontobel in [IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, Mar. 2013]. We also prove a
degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based upper bound on the permanent of a
non-negative matrix. In the limit
M\to\infty
, these lower and upper
bounds yield known Bethe-permanent-based lower and upper bounds on the
permanent of a non-negative square matrix. Similar results are obtained for
another approximation of the permanent known as the (scaled) Sinkhorn permanent.

The second main contribution is giving a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for DE-FGs in terms of finite graph covers. In general, approximating the partition function of a DE-FG is more challenging than for an S-FG because the partition function of the DE-FG is a sum of complex values and not just a sum of non-negative real values. Moreover, one cannot apply the method of types for proving the combinatorial characterization as in the case of S-FGs. We overcome this challenge by applying a suitable loop-calculus transform (LCT). Originally, the LCT was introduced by Chertkov and Chernyak as a special linear transform for S-FGs based on SPA fixed-point messages. Mori further developed the LCT for S-FGs with non-binary alphabet, but his LCT was limited to the case where SPA fixed-point messages consist of non-zero components only. In contrast, the SPA on DE-FGs often involves messages containing zeros. We introduce an alternative LCT that is applicable for both S-FGs and DE-FGs and is without the limitation of non-zero components in SPA fixed-point messages. Currently, we provide a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers for a class of DE-FGs satisfying an (easily checkable) condition. However, based on numerical results, we suspect that this combinatorial characterization holds more broadly. We further introduce the symmetric-subspace transform (SST), which reformulates the previously mentioned combinatorial characterizations for S-FGs and DE-FGs in terms of some integral. Both the LCT and the SST should be of interest beyond proving the main results of this thesis.

摘要：

各種棘手的計算問題可以歸結為計算由因子圖表示的適當定義的多變量函數的邊際和配分函數。對於與經典統計模型相關的計算問題，我們考慮標準因子圖（S-FG），即局部函數取非負實值的因子圖。對於與量子信息處理系統相關的計算問題，我們考慮雙邊因子圖（DE-FG），這是一類局部函數取複數值並滿足某些半正定約束的因子圖。乘積和算法（SPA）是一種高效的疊代方法。它可以用來逼近由這些因子圖表示的邊際和配分函數。

對於任意的S-FG，Yedidia等人顯示，SPA在此S-FG上的不動點對應於Bethe自由能函數的駐點。基於此，他們定義了Bethe配分函數近似方法，由該近似方法得到的函數我們簡稱為Bethe配分函數。Bethe配分函數是基於Bethe自由能函數的最小值來定義的。基於S-FG的有限圖覆蓋，Vontobel提供了Bethe配分函數的組合刻畫。這一刻畫的證明在很大程度上依賴於類型方法。

在本論文中，我們利用有限圖覆蓋來分析S-FG和DE-FG的SPA和Bethe配分函數。本論文有兩個主要貢獻。

第一個主要貢獻涉及一類特殊的S-FG，其中每個S-FG的配分函數等於非負方塊矩陣的積和式。這類S-FG的Bethe配分函數稱為Bethe積和式。Bethe積和式的組合刻畫由度數為M的Bethe積和式給出。其中度數為M的Bethe積和式是基於底層的S-FG的度數為M的圖覆蓋來定義的。在本論文中，我們證明了一個基於度數為M的Bethe積和式的，對於非負方塊矩陣積和式的下界，解決了Vontobel在[IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, Mar. 2013]中提出的一個推測。我們還證明了一個基於度數為M的Bethe積和式的，對於非負矩陣積和式的上界。在M趨於無窮大的極限下，這些上下界會等於已知的，基於Bethe積和式的，對於非負方塊矩陣積和式的上下界。類似的結果也適用於另一種對於積和式的近似。這種近似被稱為（縮放的）Sinkhorn積和式。

第二個主要貢獻是給出了基於DE-FG的有限圖覆蓋的，對於DE-FG的Bethe配分函數的組合刻畫。一般來說，逼近DE-FG的配分函數比S-FG的配分函數更具挑戰性，因為DE-FG的配分函數是複數的和，而不僅僅是非負實數的和。此外，我們不能直接應用類型方法來證明該組合刻畫，這跟S-FG的情況不一樣。我們通過對DE-FG應用適當的環計算變換（LCT）克服了這一困難。最初，LCT由Chertkov和Chernyak引入，作為基於SPA不動點信息的應用在S-FG上的特殊線性變換。Mori進一步發展了對於非二元字母表的S-FG的LCT，但他提出的LCT僅適用於SPA不動點的信息僅包含非零分量的情況。相比之下，DE-FG上的SPA通常涉及包含零值分量的信息。基于此，我們拓展了Mori提出的LCT。我們引入了一種同時適用於S-FG和DE-FG的LCT，並且我們提出的LCT也適用於SPA不動點信息中含有非零分量的情況。目前，我們提供了對於一類滿足（易於檢查的）條件的DE-FG的Bethe配分函數的組合刻畫。然而，基於數值仿真結果，我們推測這一組合刻畫對於廣泛的DE-FG都是成立的。我們還進一步引入了對稱子空間變換（SST），這變換可以將前述S-FG和DE-FG的組合刻畫重新表述為某些積分。除了用在證明本論文的主要結果，LCT和SST應該會有更廣泛的應用。

2.

in preparation

4.

Y. Huang, N. Kashyap, and P. O. Vontobel, “Degree-
M
Bethe and Sinkhorn permanent based bounds on the permanent of a non-negative matrix,” to appear in IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, pp. 1-19, 2024.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Factor graphs are used for representing both classical statistical models
[
KFL01
,
For01
,
Loe04
]
and quantum information processing systems
[
CV17
,
AA21
]
. For classical statistical models, we consider standard factor graphs (S-FGs)
[
KFL01
,
For01
,
Loe04
]
,
i.e.
, factor graphs with local functions taking on
non-negative real values. S-FGs have a wide range of applications across various fields, including statistical mechanics (see,
e.g.
,
[
MM09
]
), coding theory (see,
e.g.
,
[
RU08
]
), and communications (see,
e.g.
,
[
Wym07
]
). For representing quantities of interests in quantum information processing systems, we consider double-edges factor graphs (DE-FGs)
[
CV17
,
Cao21
]
,
where local functions take complex values and have to satisfy some positive semi-definiteness constraints. DE-FGs extend the concept of S-FGs and are closely related to tensor networks formalism
[
CV09
,
Coe10
,
RS19
,
AA21
]
, which is a key research area in theoretical physics
[
Cow15
]
.

Many typical inference problems can be formulated as computing the marginals and the partition function for a suitably defined multivariate function whose factorization is depicted by an S-FG
[
MM09
]
or DE-FG
[
AA21
]
. Exact computation of these quantities is, in general, challenging for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. In order to efficiently approximate these quantities, a popular approach is to apply the sum-product algorithm (SPA), also known as loopy belief propagation (LBP), on S-FGs or DE-FGs. The SPA is a heuristic algorithm that has been successfully used for approximating the marginal functions and the partition functions for various classes of S-FGs and DE-FGs.

There are various characterizations of the pseudo-marginal functions obtained via an SPA fixed point. When an S-FG or DE-FG is cycle-free (
i.e.
, tree-structured), the pseudo-marginal functions evaluated at the SPA fixed point are the exact marginal functions of the multivariate function represented by the S-FG or DE-FG (see,
e.g.
,
[
MM09
]
). For S-FGs or DE-FGs with cycles, the SPA often provides surprisingly good approximations of the marginals and partition functions. However, there exists instances of cyclic S-FGs and DE-FGs where the SPA gives a poor approximations of these quantities
[
MWJ99
,
Hes03
,
WTSJ14
]
.

For S-FGs with cycles, Yedidia
et al.
in
[
YFW05
]
defined the Bethe free energy function and also the Bethe approximation of the partition function, also known as the Bethe partition function, in terms of the minimum of the Bethe free energy function. The main idea of the Bethe free energy function dates back to the work of Bethe in 1935
[
Bet35
]
. Then Yedidia
et al.

[
YFW05
]
provided a theoretical justification of the SPA by showing that the fixed points of the SPA on an S-FG correspond to the stationary points of the Bethe free energy function. Therefore, the Bethe partition function can be computed with the help of the SPA.

For S-FGs with cycles, Chertkov and Chernyak
[
CC06
,
CC07
]

introduced a concept called loop calculus, which provides an expression
that relates the partition function to the Bethe partition function.
This expression yields an exact representation of the partition function as a finite sum of terms, with the first term being the Bethe partition function and higher-order
terms obtained by adding so-called loop corrections. Mori in
[
Mor15a
]
extended the results by Chertkov and Chernyak to S-FGs with non-binary alphabets, using concepts from information geometry.

Finite graph covers of an S-FG or DE-FG offer a different perspective to understand the SPA on these graphs. A notable observation about finite graph covers is that each cover looks locally the same as the base graph,
i.e.
, the original S-FG or DE-FG
[
KV03
]
. This implies that the SPA, a locally operating algorithm, “cannot distinguish” whether it is running on the base graph, or implicitly on any of the finite graph covers.

Finite graph covers serves as a useful theoretical tool for analyzing the SPA and the Bethe partition functions for S-FGs. Specifically:

•

In order to understand the performance of the SPA for decoding low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes, Koetter
et al.

[
KV03
,
KLVW07
]
studied finite graph covers of S-FGs representing LDPC codes. Ruozzi
et al.

[
RTT09
]
utilized finite graph covers to characterize the behavior of the max-product algorithm for Gaussian graphical models. (For Gaussian graphical models, the max-product algorithm is essentially equivalent to the SPA.)

•

Vontobel
[
Von13
]
provided a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers of an S-FG, as illustrated in Fig.
1
. In Fig.
1
,
\mathsf{N}
denotes an arbitrary S-FG,
Z(\mathsf{N})
is the partition function,
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
is the Bethe partition function, and
Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})
, the degree-
M
Bethe partition function, is defined based on the degree-
M
covers of
\mathsf{N}
.
Vontobel
[
Von13
]
showed that

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z(\mathsf{N}),\hskip 23.49976ptM=1,

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N}).

Similar characterizations were discussed in
[
AAL+17
,
ALP+22
]
.

•

Based on Vontobel’s findings
[
Von13
]
,
Ruozzi
et al.
in
[
Ruo12
]
and Csikvári
et al.
in
[
CRS22
]

proved that the partition functions are lower bounded by the Bethe partition functions
for certain classes of S-FGs, particularly resolving a conjecture by Sudderth

et al.

[
EMA07
]
regarding log-supermodular graphical models.

\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 11.74988pt\left.Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\right|_{M=1}=Z(\mathsf{N})
\hskip 14.22636ptZ_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})
\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\left.Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\right|_{M\to\infty}=Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
\latex

@@caption[A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-FG.
]A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-FG.

\latex
@@caption[A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function.]A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-FG.

In this thesis, we also leverage finite graph covers to analyze the SPA and the relationship between the Bethe partition functions and the partition functions for both S-FGs and DE-FGs.
Our investigation yields two main contributions:

1.

Degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based bounds for the permanent

of a non-negative square matrix

Vontobel in
[
Von13a
]
introduced a class of S-FGs such that the partition function of each S-FG is equal to the permanent of a non-negative square matrix. The combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for such an S-FG is given by the degree-
M
Bethe permanent, which is defined based on the degree-
M
covers of the S-FG. In this thesis, we present degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based bounds on the permanent of a non-negative square matrix, resolving a conjecture proposed by Vontobel in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture 51]
Detailed discussion can be found in Section
1.1
.

2.

Combinatorial characterization of Bethe partition function

for DE-FGs

For an arbitrary DE-FG, a natural question is whether
there is a combinatorial characterization of its Bethe partition function in terms of its finite graph covers. In this thesis, we prove this characterization for DE-FGs that satisfy an easily checkable condition. However, based on the obtained numerical results, we suspect that this characterization holds more broadly.
The detailed discussion is in Section
1.2
.

1.1
Degree-
M
Bethe and Sinkhorn Permanent Based Bounds on the Permanent of a Non-negative square Matrix

This section establishes finite-graph-cover-based bounds for the permanent of a nonnegative square matrix. In particular, Sections
1.1.1
and
1.1.2
introduce the permanent of a non-negative square matrix and the Bethe permanent, respectively, with the latter being an S-FG-based approximation of the matrix permanent. Section
1.1.3
presents the degree-
M
Bethe permanent, which is defined based on degree-
M
covers of the underlying S-FG. Section
1.1.4
then establishes degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based bounds on the matrix permanent. Moreover, Sections
1.1.2
–
1.1.4
show that similar results holds for the (scaled) Sinkhorn permanent
[
ACSS21
]
, which is another S-FG-based approximation of the matrix permanent.

1.1.1
The Permanent of a Square Matrix

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, define
[n]\triangleq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
and let

\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
be the set of all
n!
bijections from
[n]
to
[n]
. Recall
the definition of the permanent of a real square matrix (see,
e.g.
,

[
MM84
]
).

Definition 1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and let

\bm{\theta}\triangleq\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr)_{\!i,j\in[n]}
be a real matrix of
size
n\times n
. The permanent of
\bm{\theta}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\prod\limits_{i\in[n]}\theta\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr).

(1.1)

\triangle

In this thesis, we consider only the matrices
\bm{\theta}
such that there is at least one

\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
such that

\prod\limits_{i\in[n]}\theta\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\neq 0
.

Computing the exact permanent is in the complexity class #P, where #P is the
set of the counting problems associated with the decision problems in the
class NP. Notably, already the computation of the permanent
of matrices that contain only zeros and ones is #P-complete
[
Val79
]
.

1.1.2
The Bethe Permanent and the (Scaled) Sinkhorn Permanent of a Non-negative Square Matrix

While being able to compute the exact permanent of a matrix might be desirable
in some circumstances, many applications require only a good approximation of
the permanent. In this thesis, we focus on the important special
case of approximating the permanent of a non-negative real matrix,
i.e.
, a
matrix where all entries are non-negative real numbers. In particular, we
study two graphical-model-based methods for approximating the permanent of a
non-negative square matrix. The first method is motivated by Bethe-approximation /
SPA based methods proposed in
[
CKV08
,
HJ09
]
and studied in more detail in
[
Von13a
]
. The second
method is motivated by Sinkhorn’s matrix scaling algorithm
[
LSW00
]
.

The main idea of these methods is to construct an S-FG
[
For01
,
Loe04
]
whose partition
function equals the permanent.
Consequently, the permanent can be obtained by
finding the minimum of the Gibbs free energy function associated with the
factor graph. However, because finding the minimum of the Gibbs free energy
function is in general intractable unless
n
is small, one considers the
minimization of some functions that suitably approximate the Gibbs free energy
function.

A first approximation of the Gibbs free energy function is given by the Bethe
free energy function
[
YFW05
]
; the resulting approximation of

\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
is called the Bethe permanent and denoted by

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
. Vontobel
[
Von13a
]
showed that for the relevant
factor graph, the Bethe free energy function is convex and that the SPA
converges to its minimum.
(This is in contrast to the situation for factor
graphs in general, where the Bethe free energy function associated with a
factor graph is not convex and the SPA is not guaranteed to converge to its
minimum.) The Bethe permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
satisfies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}\leq 2^{n/2}.

(1.2)

The first inequality in (
1.2
) was
proven by Gurvits
[
Gur11
]
with the help of an inequality of
Schrijver
[
Sch98
]
. Later on, an alternative proof based on
results from real stable polynomials was given by Straszak and Vishnoi
in
[
SV19
]
and Anari and Gharan in
[
AG21
]
. The second
inequality in (
1.2
) was conjectured by
Gurvits
[
Gur11
]
and proven by Anari and
Rezaei
[
AR19
]
. Both inequalities
in (
1.2
) are the best possible given
only the knowledge of the size of the matrix
\bm{\theta}
.

A second approximation of the Gibbs free energy function is given by the
Sinkhorn free energy function; the resulting approximation of
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})

is called the Sinkhorn permanent and denoted by
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\theta})
. (The
Sinkhorn free energy function was formally defined in
[
Von14
]

based on some considerations in
[
HJ09
]
. Note that

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\theta})
appears already in
[
LSW00
]
as an approximation
of
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
without being called the Sinkhorn permanent.) The
Sinkhorn free energy function is easily seen to be convex and it can be
efficiently minimized with the help of Sinkhorn’s matrix scaling
algorithm
[
LSW00
]
, thereby motivating its name. In this thesis, we
will actually work with the scaled Sinkhorn permanent

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})\triangleq\mathrm{e}^{-n}\cdot\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\theta})
, a variant of the
Sinkhorn permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{S}}(\bm{\theta})
that was introduced
in
[
AG21
]
. The scaled Sinkhorn permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})

satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot\frac{n!}{n^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}\leq\mathrm{e}^{n}.

(1.3)

The first inequality in (
1.3
)
follows from van der Waerden’s inequality proven by
Egorychev
[
Ego81
]
and Falikman
[
Fal81
]
, whereas the
second equality follows from a relatively simple to prove inequality. Both
inequalities in (
1.3
) are the
best possible given only the knowledge of the size of the
matrix
\bm{\theta}
. Note that the value on the LHS
of (
1.3
) is approximately

\sqrt{2\pi n}
.

1.1.3
A Combinatorial Characterization of the Bethe Permanent and the (Scaled) Sinkhorn Permanent

While the definition of
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
in (
1.1
) clearly
has a combinatorial flavor (
e.g.
, counting weighted perfect matchings in a
complete bipartite graph with two times
n
vertices), it is, a priori, not
clear if a combinatorial characterization can be given for
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

and
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
. However, this is indeed the case.

Namely, using the results in
[
Von13
]
, a finite-graph-cover-based
combinatorial characterization was given for
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

in
[
Von13a
]
. Namely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\limsup_{M\to\infty}\,\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta}),

where the degree-
M
Bethe permanent is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt[M]{\bigl\langle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}})\bigr\rangle_{\bm{P}_{M}\in\tilde{\Psi}_{M}}},

where the angular brackets represent an arithmetic average and where

\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}}
represents a degree-
M
cover of
\bm{\theta}

defined by a collection of permutation matrices
\bm{P}_{M}
. (See
Definition
28
for the details.)

In this thesis we offer a combinatorial characterization of the scaled Sinkhorn
permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
. Namely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\limsup_{M\to\infty}\,\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta}),

where
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
was defined
in
[
ACSS21
]
, and where
the degree-
M
scaled Sinkhorn permanent is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}\bigl(\langle\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}}\rangle_{\bm{P}_{M}\in\tilde{\Psi}_{M}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}\otimes\bm{U}_{M,M})},

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product of two matrices and where

\bm{U}_{M,M}
is the matrix of size
M\times M
with all entries equal to

1/M
. (See Definition
31
and
Proposition
32
for the details.)
1
1

1

Note that the quantity

\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}\otimes\bm{U}_{M,M})}
appears in the literature at
least as early as
[
Ban76
,
Fri79
]
. These papers show that for

M=2^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, it holds that

\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}\otimes\bm{U}_{M,M})}\leq\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
, which,
in our notation, implies
1\leq\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
for

M=2^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
2
2

2

Barvinok
[
Bar10
]
considered combinatorial expressions
in the same spirit as
\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}\otimes\bm{U}_{M,M})}
, but
the details and the limit are different.

\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 11.74988pt\left.\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})\right|_{M=1}=\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\left.\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})\right|_{M\to\infty}=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 11.74988pt\left.\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})\right|_{M=1}=\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\left.\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})\right|_{M\to\infty}=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
\latex

@@caption[Combinatorial characterizations of the
Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn permanents.]Combinatorial characterizations of the
Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn permanents.
\latex
@@caption[Combinatorial characterizations of the
Bethe and Sinkhorn permanents]Combinatorial characterizations of the
Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn permanents.

These combinatorial characterizations of the Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn
permanent are summarized in Fig.
1.1.3
.

Besides the quantities
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
being
of inherent interest, they are particularly interesting toward understanding
the ratios

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})},\hskip 23.49976pt\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}

for general non-negative square matrices or for
special classes of non-negative square matrices.

For example, as discussed in
[
Von13
, Sec. VII]
, one can consider
the equation

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317pt\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}\!\cdot\!\frac{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},3}(\bm{\theta})}\!\cdot\!\frac{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},3}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},4}(\bm{\theta})}\!\cdots.

If one can give bounds on the ratios appearing on the RHS of this equation,
then one obtains bounds on the ratio on the LHS of this equation.

Alternatively, one can consider the equation

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}}_{\text{➀}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}}_{\text{➁}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}}_{\text{➂}}.

(1.4)

Based on numerical and analytical results, Ng and Vontobel
[
NV22
]

concluded that it appears that for many classes of matrices of interest, the
ratio ➁ behaves similarly to the ratio ➂. Therefore,
understanding the ratio ➁ goes a long way toward understanding the
ratio ➀. (Note that the ratio ➁ is easier to analyze than
the ratio ➀ for some classes of matrices of interest.)

Results similar in spirit to the above results were given for the Bethe
approximation of the partition function of other graphical models (see,
e.g.
,

[
Ruo12
,
Von16
,
CRS22
]
).

1.1.4
Main Contributions

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
\bm{\theta}
be a fixed non-negative matrix of size

n\times n
, and let
\Gamma_{M,n}
be the set of doubly stochastic matrices of
size
n\times n
where all entries are integer multiples of
1/M
. Moreover,
for
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
, let

\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{i,j\in[n]}\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr)^{\!M\cdot\gamma(i,j)}
. (Note that in this expression, all exponents
are non-negative integers.) Throughout this thesis, we consider
\bm{\theta}

such that
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
,
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
, and

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
are positive real-valued.

Lemma 2
.

There are collections of non-negative real numbers

\bigl\{C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr\}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}
,​

\bigl\{C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr\}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}
,​

\bigl\{C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr\}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}
such that

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma}),

(1.5)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma}),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma}).

(1.7)

\square

With the help of the inequalities
in (
1.2
)
and (
1.3
), along with some
results that are established in this thesis, we can make the following
statement about the coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,
C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
, and

C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
in Lemma
2
.

Theorem 3
.

For every
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
, the coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
, and
C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
satisfy

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}\leq\Bigl(2^{n/2}\Bigr)^{\!M-1},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\Biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\Biggr)^{\!\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}\leq\Biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\Biggr)^{\!\!n}.

(1.9)

\square

Combining Lemma
2
and
Theorem
3
, we can make the
following statement.

Theorem 4
.

It holds that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})}\leq\Bigl(2^{n/2}\Bigr)^{\!\!\frac{M-1}{M}},

(1.10)

\displaystyle\frac{M^{n}}{(M!)^{n/M}}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!\frac{M-1}{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})}\leq\frac{M^{n}}{(M!)^{n/M}}.

(1.11)

Note that in the limit
M\to\infty
we recover the inequalities
in (
1.2
)
and (
1.3
).

\square

Finally, we discuss the following asymptotic characterization of the
coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,
C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
, and

C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. It holds that

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})+o(M)\bigr),

(1.12)

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})+o(M)\bigr),

(1.13)

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})+o(M)\bigr),

(1.14)

where
H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})
,
H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})
, and
H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})
are the
modified Gibbs entropy function, the Bethe entropy function, and the
scaled Sinkhorn entropy function, respectively, as defined in Section
2.4
.

\square

Let us put the above results into some context:

•

The first inequality in (
1.8
) proves the first (weaker)
conjecture in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture 52]
.

•

Smarandache and Haenggi
[
SH15
]
claimed a proof of the
second (stronger) conjecture
in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture 52]
,
3
3

3

The second
(stronger) conjecture in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture 52]
stated that
for each
\bm{P}_{M}\in\tilde{\Psi}_{M}
, there is a collection of
non-negative real numbers

\bigl\{C_{\mathrm{B},M,n,\bm{P}_{M}}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr\}_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}

such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}})=\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{B},M,n,\bm{P}_{M}}(\bm{\gamma}),

and that for every
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
, it holds that

C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})/C_{\mathrm{B},M,n,\bm{P}_{M}}(\bm{\gamma})\geq 1.

which would imply the first (weaker) conjecture in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture
52]
. However, the proof in
[
SH15
]
is
incomplete.

•

The first inequality in (
1.10
) proves the first (weaker)
conjecture in
[
Von13a
, Conjecture 51]
.

•

The proof of the first inequality in (
1.8
), and with that
the proof of the first inequality in (
1.10
), uses an
inequality of
Schrijver
[
Sch98
]
. By now, there are various
proofs of Schrijver’s inequality and also various proofs of the first
inequality in (
1.2
) available in the
literature, however, the ones that we are aware of are either quite involved
(like Schrijver’s original proof of his inequality) or use reasonably
sophisticated machinery like real stable polynomials. Given the established
veracity of the first inequality in (
1.10
), it is hoped that
a more basic proof for this inequality, and with that of the first
inequality in (
1.2
), can be
found. Similar statements can be made for the other inequalities
in (
1.10
) and (
1.11
).

In fact, for
M=2
, a basic proof of the inequalities
in (
1.10
) is available: it is based
on
[
NV22
, Proposition 2]
(which appears in this thesis as
Proposition
70
), along with the observation
that the quantity
c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
appearing therein is bounded as

0\leq c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\leq n/2
. Another basic proof of the first
inequality in (
1.10
) is based on a straightforward
generalization of the result in
[
Csi17
, Lemma 4.2]
from

\{0,1\}
-valued matrices to arbitrary non-negative square matrices and then
averaging over
2
-covers.

Moreover, for the case where
\bm{\theta}
is proportional to the all-one matrix
and where
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is arbitrary, a basic proof of the first inequality
in (
1.10
) was given in
[
Von13a
, Appendix I]
.
However, presently it is not clear how to generalize this proof to other
matrices
\bm{\theta}
.

•

For
M=2
, the lower and upper bounds in (
1.10
) are
exactly the square root of the lower and upper bounds
in (
1.2
), respectively, thereby
corroborating some of the statements made after (
1.4
).

•

Although we take advantage of special properties of the factor graphs
under consideration, potentially the techniques in this thesis can be
extended toward a better understanding of the Bethe approximation of the
partition function of other factor graphs. (For more on this topic, see the discussion in Chapter
7
.)

1.2
Characterizing the Bethe Partition Function of

Double-Edges Factor Graphs via Graph Covers

The results in Section
1.1
are for factor graphs called S-FGs,
i.e.
, for factor graphs with local functions taking on non-negative real values. Recently, several papers
[
LV12
,
LV17
,
LV20
,
Mor15
]
have explored more general factor graphs, in particular those where the local functions take on complex values. This exploration is particularly relevant for representing quantities of interest in quantum information processing. (For connections of these factor graphs to other graphical notations in physics, see
[
LV17
, Appendix A]
.) The structure of these factor graphs is not completely arbitrary. In order to formalize such factor graphs, Cao and Vontobel
[
CV17
]
introduced DE-FGs, where local functions take on complex values and have to satisfy certain positive semi-definiteness constraints. They
[
CV17
]
further showed that the partition function of a DE-FG is a non-negative real number. However, because the partition function is the sum of complex numbers (notably, the real and the imaginary parts of these complex numbers can have a positive or negative sign), approximating the partition function is, in general, much more challenging for DE-FGs than for S-FGs. This problem is known as the numerical sign problem in applied mathematics and theoretical physics (see,
e.g.
,
[
LGS+90
]
).

Based on the positive semi-definiteness constraints posed for DE-FGs, Cao and Vontobel
[
Cao21
]
developed the SPA for DE-FGs, where the SPA messages also satisfy these constraints, and further defined the Bethe partition function based on the SPA fixed-point messages.
4
4

4

Similar to the definition of the Bethe partition function in
[
CV17
, Def. 8]
, in this thesis, the Bethe partition function is formulated based on the so-called pseudo-dual Bethe free energy function for S-FGs. Although
generalizing the primal Bethe free energy function from S-FGs to DE-FGs is
formally straightforward, it poses challenges because of the
multi-valuedness of the complex logarithm and is left for future research.

Then they showed that the Bethe partition function is a non-negative real number.

DE-FGs have a strong connection to tensor networks formulism
[
CV09
,
Coe10
,
RS19
,
AA21
]
, which has become a central topic in theoretical physics
[
Cow15
]
. In
[
AA21
]
, Alkabetz and Arad showed how to map a projected-entangled-pair-state (PEPS) tensor network to a DE-FG.
Alkabetz and Arad in
[
AA21
]
connected the SPA for DE-FGs to tensor network contractions. Motivated by this connection, Tindall and Fishman
[
TF23
]
related the SPA for DE-FGs to known tensor network gauging methods. Moreover, Tindall
et al.

[
TFSS24
]
showed that for the kicked Ising quantum system studied in
[
KEA+23
]
, the SPA-based approximation results are more accurate and precise than those obtained from the quantum processor and many other classical methods. These promising results motivate us to study the SPA for DE-FGs.

Given that both the partition function and the Bethe partition function are non-negative real numbers for an arbitrary DE-FG
[
CV17
]
, the existence of a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers for DE-FGs remains an open question. Numerical results on small DE-FGs suggested that such a characterization might exist. However, because in general, the partition function of a DE-FG is a sum of complex numbers, proving the combinatorial characterization for DE-FGs turns out to be much more challenging than for S-FGs. The method of types, which was the main ingredient in the proof of the combinatorial characterization for S-FGs
[
Von13
]
, cannot be directly adapted to the proof for DE-FGs. The challenges of approximating the sum of complex numbers are illustrated by the following example.

Example 6
.

For any positive-valued integer
M
and any complex number
\alpha
, we define

\displaystyle Z_{M}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{M}c_{M,\ell},

(1.15)

where

\displaystyle c_{M,\ell}=\binom{M}{\ell}\cdot(1-\alpha)^{M-\ell}\cdot\alpha^{\ell}.

Using the binomial theorem, one can verify that
Z_{M}=1
for any positive-valued integer
M
and any complex number
\alpha
.

If
\alpha
is a non-negative number satisfying
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, then
c_{M,\ell}
is non-negative for all
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\}
, and we obtain

\displaystyle\max\limits_{\ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}c_{M,\ell}\leq Z_{M}\leq(M+1)\cdot\max\limits_{\ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}c_{M,\ell},

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log( \max\limits_{ \ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\} } c_{M,\ell} )

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log(Z_{M})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log(M+1)+\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log( \max\limits_{ \ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\} } c_{M,\ell} ).

In the limit
M\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log(Z_{M})=\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log( \max\limits_{ \ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\} } c_{M,\ell} ).

(1.16)

For simplicity of exposition, we suppose that there exists a positive-valued integer
M
such that
\alpha\cdot M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Then for such an
\alpha
,
we have

\displaystyle\max\limits_{\ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}c_{M,\ell}=c_{M,\alpha\cdot M}=\hskip-14.22636pt\underbrace{\binom{M}{\alpha\cdot M}}_{\overset{(a)}{=}\exp( M \cdot h(\alpha) + o(M) )}\hskip-19.91684pt\cdot\underbrace{(1-\alpha)^{M(1-\alpha)}\cdot\alpha^{M\alpha}}_{=\exp( -M \cdot h(\alpha) )}=\exp( o(M) ),

(1.17)

where
h(\alpha)\triangleq-\alpha\cdot\log( \alpha)-(1-\alpha)\cdot\log( 1 - \alpha)
,
where
o(M)
is the usual little-
o
notation for a function in
M
,
and where step
(a)
follows from Stirling’s approximation (see, e.g.,
[
Rob55
]
).
Plugging (
1.17
) into (
1.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log(Z_{M})=\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\cdot\log( \max\limits_{ \ell\in\{0,\ldots,M\} } c_{M,\ell} )=\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{o(M)}{M}=0.

We can see that for the case
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, all the terms in
Z_{M}
are non-negative and are added up constructively, allowing us to approximate
Z_{M}
by the term with the largest magnitude. Following a similar idea, Vontobel
[
Von13
]

used the method of types to characterize the valid configurations in finite graph covers of an S-FG, and he further gave a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function of the S-FG.

However, if
\alpha
is a complex-valued number with non-zero imaginary part, the terms in the expression of
Z_{M}
in (
1.15
), might take values in the field of complex numbers, and these terms are added up constructively and destructively. In this case,
we cannot approximate
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}M^{-1}\cdot\log(Z_{M})
using the same idea as in the case
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, as the term with the largest magnitude gives a poor estimate of
Z_{M}
.

1.2.1
Main Contributions

We develop a novel approach to give a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition functions for a certain class of DE-FGs. This novel proof approach is one of the main results in this thesis, besides the statement itself.

Our proof approach consists of three main ingredients:

1.

At the first step, we apply the loop-calculus transform (LCT)
5
5

5

The loop-calculus transform can be seen as a particular instance of a holographic
transform
[
AM11
]
.
to a DE-FG based on a
fixed point of the SPA. (If the SPA has multiple fixed points,
we assume that it is the fixed point with the largest Bethe partition
function value.) The benefit of the LCT is that the
transformed SPA fixed-point messages have an elementary form, which is independent of the edge and the direction of a message. Note that the LCT leaves the partition function of the DE-FG unchanged.

2.

At the second step, for any positive-valued integer
M
, we construct the average degree-
M
cover for the transformed DE-FG. Then we express the average of the partition functions of the degree-
M
covers in terms of the partition function of this average degree-
M
cover.

3.

At the third step, we evaluate the partition function of this average degree-
M
cover in the limit
M\to\infty
.
So far, we can relate the limit of this partition function to the Bethe partition function when some (easily checkable) conditions are satisfied. However, based on the obtained numerical results, we suspect that this combinatorial characterization holds more generally. We leave it as an open problem to broadly characterize when the DE-FGs do not satisfy the (easily checkable) conditions.

Based on finite graph covers of a DE-FG, we further develop the symmetric-subspace transform (SST), which allows one to express the partition function of the average degree-
M
cover of the DE-FG in terms of some integral.
Both the LCT and the SST should be of interest beyond proving the main results in this thesis. The reasons are listed as follows.

1.

The LCT introduced in this thesis is based on SPA fixed-point messages consisting of complex-valued components, which is applicable for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. This contrasts with the LCT proposed in
[
Mor15a
]
, which requires SPA fixed-point messages to be non-zero valued. Therefore, our proposed transform generalizes the one presented in
[
Mor15a
]
.

2.

The SST defined in this thesis is applicable for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first time that this transform is introduced in factor-graph literature, although it has wide applications
in quantum physics
[
WBC15
,
Har13
]
.

1.3
Structure of this Thesis

This thesis is structured as follows. Chapter
2
reviews the basic definitions and notations that are frequently used in this thesis. In particular, Chapter
2
introduces S-FGs, a special class of S-FGs such that the partition function of each S-FG is equal to the permanent of a non-negative square matrix, DE-FGs, and finite graph covers of these factor graphs. Chapter
3
proves degree-
M
-Bethe-permanent-based bounds on the permanent of a non-negative square matrix and also similar results for another approximation of the permanent known as the (scaled) Sinkhorn permanent. Chapters
4
and
5
introduce the LCT and the SST, respectively, for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. Chapter
6
proves the graph-cover theorem,
i.e.
, a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers, for a class of DE-FGs satisfying an easily checkable condition. Various (longer) proofs have been collected in the appendices.

Chapter 2
Preliminaries

In this chapter, we introduce the preliminaries of this thesis. We begin by introducing the basic definitions and notations used throughout this thesis in Section
2.1
. Next, we review the fundamental concepts of standard normal factor graphs (S-NFGs) in Section
2.2
. In Section
2.3
, we study a class of S-NFGs such that the partition function of each of these S-NFGs equals the permanent of a non-negative square matrix. Moving to the quantum case, we introduce double-edge normal factor graphs (DE-NFGs) in Section
2.5
. Finally, in Section
2.6
, we present the finite graph covers of the factor graphs introduced in this chapter.

2.1
Basic Definition and Notations

If not mentioned otherwise, all variable alphabets are assumed to be finite.
We will use, without essential loss of generality, normal
factor graphs (NFGs),
i.e.
, factor graphs where variables are associated with
edges
[
For01
,
Loe04
]
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times n}
. We define
\operatorname{supp}(\bm{\theta})
to be the support of
\bm{\theta}
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]\bigm|\theta(i,j)>0\bigr\}.

Consider two finite subsets
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
of
[n]
such that

|\mathcal{I}|=|\mathcal{J}|
. The set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
is
defined to be the set of all bijections from
\mathcal{I}
to
\mathcal{J}
. In
particular, if
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}
, then the set

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
represents the set of all permutations of the
elements in
\mathcal{I}
, and we simply write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
instead of

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
. Given

\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
, we define the matrix

\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{J}}
to be

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(P_{\sigma}(i,j)\bigr)_{\!(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{J}},\ \ \ P_{\sigma}(i,j)\triangleq\begin{cases}1&\sigma(i)=j\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Note that
\bm{P}_{\sigma}
is a permutation matrix,
i.e.
, a matrix where all
entries are equal to
0
except for exactly one
1
per row and exactly one

1
per column. Moreover, given a set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
, the set

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
is defined to be the set of the associated
permutation matrices,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\bigm|\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}\bigr\}.

(2.1)

If
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}
, we simply write
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
instead of

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{J}}
. A particularly important special case for this
thesis is the choice
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}=[n]
: the set
\mathcal{P}_{[n]}

represents the set of all permutation matrices of size
n\times n
where the
rows and columns are indexed by
[n]
.

We define
\Gamma_{n}
to be the set of all doubly stochastic
matrices of size
n\times n
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\bm{\gamma}=\bigl(\gamma(i,j)\bigr)_{\!i,j\in[n]}\ \middle|\ \begin{array}[]{l}\gamma(i,j)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\,\forall i,j\in[n]\\
\sum\limits_{j\in[n]}\gamma(i,j)=1,\,\forall i\in[n]\\
\sum\limits_{i\in[n]}\gamma(i,j)=1,\,\forall j\in[n]\end{array}\right\}.

Moreover, for
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we define
\Gamma_{M,n}
to be the subset of

\Gamma_{n}
that contains all matrices where the entries are multiples of

1/M
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M,n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}\bigm|M\cdot\gamma(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ \forall i,j\in[n]\bigr\}.

Observe that
\Gamma_{1,n}=\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
.

All logarithms in this thesis are natural logarithms and the value of

0\cdot\log(0)
is defined to be
0
.

In this thesis, given an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, if there is no ambiguity,
we use
\prod\limits_{i,j}
,
\prod\limits_{i}
,
\prod\limits_{j}
,
\sum\limits_{i,j}
,

\sum\limits_{i}
, and
\sum\limits_{j}
for
\prod\limits_{i,j\in[n]}
,
\prod\limits_{i\in[n]}
,

\prod\limits_{j\in[n]}
,
\sum\limits_{i,j\in[n]}
,
\sum\limits_{i\in[n]}
, and

\sum\limits_{j\in[n]}
, respectively.

2.2
Standard Normal Factor Graphs (S-NFGs)

In this section, we review some basic concepts and properties of an S-NFG. We use an example to introduce the key concepts of an S-NFG.

\latex

@@caption[NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Example
7
.]NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Example
7
.
\latex
@@caption[NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Example
7
.]NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Example
7
.

x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{4}
x_{5}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
\latex

@@caption[]
\latex
@@caption[]

Example 7
.

Consider the multivariate function

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{5})\triangleq f_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\cdot f_{2}(x_{1},x_{4})\cdot f_{3}(x_{2},x_{5})\cdot f_{4}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}).

where
g
, the
so-called global function, is defined to be the product of the so-called
local functions
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
. We can visualize the factorization of
g
with the help of the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
in
Fig.
2.2
. Note that the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
shown in
Fig.
2.2
consists of four function nodes

f_{1},\ldots,f_{4}
and five (full) edges with associated variables

x_{1},\ldots,x_{5}
.

\square

An edge that connects to two function nodes is called a full edge, whereas an edge
that is connected to only one function node is called a half edge. For the
purposes of this thesis, it is sufficient to consider only S-NFGs with full
edges, because S-NFGs with half edges can be turned into S-NFGs with only full
edges by attaching a dummy
1
-valued function node to the other end of every half edge without changing any marginals or the partition function.

Definition 8
.

An S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
consists of the following
objects:

1.

The graph
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
with vertex set
\mathcal{F}
and edge set

\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{F}
is also known as the set of function nodes. (As
mentioned above, every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
will be assumed to be a full
edge connecting two function nodes.)

2.

The alphabet
\mathcal{X}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{X}_{e}
, where

\mathcal{X}_{e}
is the alphabet of the variable
x_{e}
associated with the edge

e\in\mathcal{E}
. In this thesis, without loss of generality, we suppose that
0\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

For a given S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
, we fix the order of the function nodes by
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{|\mathcal{F}|}\}.
Then we make the following definitions.

3.

For every function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the set
\partial f
is defined to be the set of edges incident on
f
.

4.

For every edge
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
, the pair
(f_{i},f_{j})
is defined to be the pair of function nodes that are connected to edge
e
such that
i<j
. The set
\partial e
is defined to be the set of function nodes incident on
e
, i.e.,
\partial e\triangleq\{f_{i},f_{j}\}
. Note that for notational convenience, here we impose a direction on every edge
(f_{i},f_{j})
, i.e., the inequality
i<j
, that is irrelevant for our results.

5.

For any finite set
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{X}_{e},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq(x_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}.

6.

An assignment
\bm{x}\triangleq(x_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\in\mathcal{X}
is called a
configuration of the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, a configuration

\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
induces the vector
\bm{x}_{\partial f}\triangleq(x_{e})_{e\in\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}
.

7.

For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the local function associated with
f
is,
with some slight abuse of notation, also called
f
. Here, the local
function
f
is an arbitrary mapping from
\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}
to

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

8.

The global function
g
is defined to be the mapping

\displaystyle g:\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{x}\mapsto\prod\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}f(\bm{x}_{\partial f}).

9.

A configuration
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
satisfying
g(\bm{x})\neq 0
is called a
valid configuration. The set of valid configurations is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\triangleq\{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}\ |\ g(\bm{x})\neq 0\}.

10.

The partition function is defined to be

Z(\mathsf{N})\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{C}}g(\bm{x})
.
1
1

1

In this thesis, the
partition function
Z(\mathsf{N})
of
\mathsf{N}
is a scalar,
i.e.
, it
is not really a function. If
\mathsf{N}
depends on some parameter
(say, some temperature parameter), then
Z(\mathsf{N})
is a function of
that parameter.
Clearly, the partition function satisfies

Z(\mathsf{N})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

\triangle

In this thesis, we will only consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
for which

Z(\mathsf{N})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

If there is no ambiguity, when we consider S-NFG, we will use the short-hands

\sum\limits_{\bm{x}}
,

\sum\limits_{x_{e}}
,

\sum\limits_{e}
,

\sum\limits_{f}
,

\prod\limits_{\bm{x}}
,

\prod\limits_{x_{e}}
,

\prod\limits_{e}
,

\prod\limits_{f}
,

(\cdot)_{x_{e}}
,
and

(\cdot)_{e}
for

\sum\limits_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
,

\sum\limits_{x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}}
,

\sum\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,

\sum\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
,

\prod\limits_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}
,

\prod\limits_{x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}}
,

\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,

\prod\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
,

(\cdot)_{x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}}
,
and

(\cdot)_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,
respectively.
For any set
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, we will use the short-hands

\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
and
(\cdot)_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}}

for

\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
and
(\cdot)_{\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}}
.
Moreover,
\partial f\setminus e
will be short-hand notation for

\partial f\setminus\{e\}
.

Now we introduce the Gibbs free energy function, which provides an alternative expression of the partition function.

Definition 9
.

(The Gibbs free energy function)

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and
\bm{p}\triangleq\bigl(p(\bm{x})\bigr)_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{C}}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}
.
The Gibbs free energy function is defined to be the mapping
(see, e.g.,
[
Von13
, Definition 9]
)

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{G}}:\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{p}

\displaystyle\mapsto U_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})-H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p}),

where

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{G}}:\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle\bm{p}

\displaystyle\mapsto-\sum\limits_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{C}}p(\bm{x})\cdot\log\bigl(g(\bm{x})\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{G}}:\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle\bm{p}

\displaystyle\mapsto-\sum\limits_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{C}}p(\bm{x})\cdot\log\bigl(p(\bm{x})\bigr).

\triangle

Proposition 10
.

It holds that
F_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})
is a convex function w.r.t.

\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}
and

\displaystyle Z(\mathsf{N})=\exp( - \min_{ \bm{p} \in\Pi_{\set{C}} } F_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p}) ).

Proof.

See,
e.g.
,
[
Von13
, Lemma 8]
.
∎

In general, neither the direct evaluation of the partition function nor solving the convex optimization problem in Proposition
10
leads to a tractable computational problem, except for small-scaled S-NFG.
This limitation motivates the study of alternative approaches, specifically approximations of the partition function and the Gibbs free energy function.

In the following, we introduce the SPA, whose fixed points often provide decent approximation to the partition function. Note that we only give the technical details of the SPA on an S-NFG; for a general discussion with respect to (w.r.t.) the motivations behind the SPA, see,
e.g.
,
[
KFL01
,
Loe04
]
.

Definition 11
.

(The SPA on S-NFG)

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. The SPA is an iterative algorithm that
sends messages along edges, where messages are functions over the alphabet
associated with an edge. (Note that for every iteration and every edge, two
messages are sent along that edge, one in both directions.) More precisely,
the SPA consists of the following steps:

1.

(Initialization) For every
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the
message
\mu_{e,f}^{(0)}:\mathcal{X}_{e}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, is defined to be some
arbitrary function. (Typically,
\mu_{e,f}^{(0)}(x_{e})\triangleq 1/|\mathcal{X}_{e}|

for all
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
.)

2.

(Iteration) For
t=1,2,3,\ldots
, do the following calculations
until some termination criterion is met:
2
2

2

The termination
criterion is typically a combination of numerical convergence and an
upper bound on the number of iterations.

(a)

For every
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
, we define the scaling factor
\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}
to be

\displaystyle\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f_{i}}}f_{i}(\bm{x}_{\partial f_{i}})\cdot\prod\limits_{e^{\prime}\in\partial f_{i}\setminus e}\mu_{e^{\prime},f_{i}}^{(t-1)}(x_{e^{\prime}}).

One can obtain the definition of
\kappa_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}
by switching
f_{i}
with
f_{j}
in the above definition.

(b)

If

\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\kappa_{e,f}^{(t)}=0
,
we randomly generate each element in
\bm{\mu}^{(t)}
following uniform distribution in
[0,1]
.

(c)

If

\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\kappa_{e,f}^{(t)}\neq 0
, for every
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
we define the messages from the edge
e=(f_{i},f_{j})
to the function node

f_{j}
via the mapping:
3
3

3

If desired or necessary, other generalization procedures is allowed.

\displaystyle\mu_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}:\,\mathcal{X}_{e}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle z_{e}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\!-1}\cdot\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f_{i}}:\,x_{e}=z_{e}}f_{i}(\bm{x}_{\partial f_{i}})\cdot\prod\limits_{e^{\prime}\in\partial f_{i}\setminus e}\mu_{e^{\prime},f_{i}}^{(t-1)}(x_{e^{\prime}}).

(2.2)

One can obtain the definition of
\mu_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}
by switching
f_{i}
with
f_{j}
in (
2.2
).

(d)

For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we define

\displaystyle\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(x_{e}).

If
\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\neq 0
, then we define the belief function at function node
f
to be the mapping

\displaystyle\beta_{f}^{(t)}:\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{x}_{\partial f}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\!-1}\cdot f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(x_{e}).

(2.3)

The associated vector is defined to be

\bm{\beta}_{f}^{(t)}\triangleq\bigl(\beta_{f}^{(t)}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\bigr)_{\!\bm{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}
.

(e)

For every
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we define

\displaystyle\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\triangleq\sum\limits_{x_{e}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(x_{e}).

If
\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\neq 0
, then we define the belief function at edge
e
to be the mapping

\displaystyle\beta_{e}^{(t)}:\mathcal{X}_{e}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\hskip 11.74988ptx_{e}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\bigr)^{-1}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}(x_{e})\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}(x_{e}).

(2.4)

The vector
\bm{\beta}_{e}^{(t)}
is defined to be
\bm{\beta}_{e}^{(t)}\triangleq\bigl(\beta_{e}^{(t)}(x_{e})\bigr)_{\!x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}}
.

3.

At iteration
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the SPA messages can be collected as the SPA message vector:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{(t)}\triangleq\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}\bigr)_{\!e\in\partial f,\,f\in\mathcal{F}},

where
\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}\triangleq\bigl(\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(x_{e})\bigr)_{\!x_{e}}
.
With this, the SPA message update rules can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{(t)}=f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(t-1)}\bigr)

for some suitably defined function
f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}
.

4.

The beliefs at function nodes and edges can be collected as the following vector:

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}^{(t)}\triangleq\Bigl((\bm{\beta}_{f}^{(t)})_{f\in\mathcal{F}},\,(\bm{\beta}_{e}^{(t)})_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\Bigr).

5.

An SPA message vector

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}\triangleq(\bm{\mu}_{e,f})_{e\in\partial f,\,f\in\mathcal{F}}

(2.5)

with

\bm{\mu}_{e,f}\triangleq\bigl(\mu_{e,f}(x_{e})\bigr)_{\!x_{e}}

is called an SPA fixed-point message vector if

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}=f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}(\bm{\mu}).

(2.6)

(If desired or necessary, other normalization procedures for messages and
beliefs can be specified.)

\triangle

Definition 12
.

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and let

\bm{\mu}
be some SPA fixed-point
message vector for
\mathsf{N}
as defined in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
). The collection of the beliefs

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}\triangleq\Bigl((\bm{\beta}_{f})_{f\in\mathcal{F}},\,(\bm{\beta}_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\Bigr)

(2.7)

induced by the SPA fixed-point messages is computed as follows with

\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}=\bm{\mu}_{e,f}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
:

1.

for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, if

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}(x_{e})>0,

then the elements in the vector
\bm{\beta}_{f}
are
obtained by letting
\bm{\beta}_{f}=\bm{\beta}_{f}^{(t)}
, where the elements in
\bm{\beta}_{f}^{(t)}
are given in (
2.3
);

2.

for every
e\in\mathcal{E}
, if

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x_{e}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}(x_{e})>0,

(2.8)

then the elements in the vector
\bm{\beta}_{e}

are obtained by letting
\bm{\beta}_{e}=\bm{\beta}_{e}^{(t)}
, where the elements in
\bm{\beta}_{e}^{(t)}
are given in (
2.4
).

\triangle

Assumption 13
.

In the context of S-NFG, if not mentioned otherwise, we will only consider the following normalization procedure for the SPA
message vectors
\bm{\mu}^{(t)}
and the collection of the beliefs
\bm{\beta}^{(t)}
:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{e}},\hskip 23.49976pte\in\partial f,\,f\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}^{(t)}_{e}

\displaystyle\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{e}},\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}^{(t)}_{f}

\displaystyle\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}},\hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F}.

Furthermore, we only consider the SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
such that
the strict inequality in (
2.8
)
holds for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

(See Item
3
in Remark
21
for a non-trivial S-NFG with an SPA fixed-point message vector such that the inequality in (
2.8
) does not satisfy for some
e\in\mathcal{E}
.)

\square

Proposition 14
.

[
YFW05
, Proposition 5]

For the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
such that
f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for all
\bm{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
, each SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
satisfies the inequality (
2.8
) for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and the inequality (
2.8
) for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

\square

Now we introduce the Bethe free energy function, which gives an S-FG-based approximation to the Gibbs free energy function. Proposition
10
states that the value of the partition function can be evaluated by the minimum of the Gibbs free energy function. Therefore, the minimum of the Bethe free energy function yields an approximation to the partition function. The following definitions are adapted from
[
YFW05
, Section V]
to S-NFGs.

Definition 15
.

​
[
YFW05
, Section V]

Let
\mathsf{N}
be some S-NFG. Recall that
the collection of the beliefs
\bm{\beta}
is defined in (
2.7
).
The local marginal polytope
\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})

associated with
\mathsf{N}
is defined to be the following set:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\bm{\beta}\ \middle|\ \begin{array}[]{c}\bm{\beta}_{f}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}\ \text{for all}\ f\in\mathcal{F}\\
\bm{\beta}_{e}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}\ \text{for all}\ e\in\mathcal{E}\\[2.84544pt]
\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}:\,x_{e}=z_{e}}\beta_{f}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})=\beta_{e}(z_{e})\\
\ \text{for all}\ e\in\partial f,f\in\mathcal{F},z_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}\end{array}\right\}.

\triangle

Definition 16
.

​
[
YFW05
, Section V]

Let
\mathsf{N}
be some S-NFG. The Bethe free energy function
is defined to be the following mapping:

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta}):\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\beta}\mapsto U_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta})-H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta}),

where

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta}):\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\beta}\mapsto\sum\limits_{f}U_{\mathrm{B},f}(\bm{\beta}_{f}),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta}):\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\beta}\mapsto\sum\limits_{f}H_{\mathrm{B},f}(\bm{\beta}_{f})-\sum\limits_{e}H_{\mathrm{B},e}(\bm{\beta}_{e}),

with

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{B},f}(\bm{\beta}_{f}):\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{f}\mapsto-\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}\beta_{f}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\log\bigl(f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{B},f}(\bm{\beta}_{f}):\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{f}\mapsto-\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}\beta_{f}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\log\bigl(\beta_{f}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{B},e}(\bm{\beta}_{e}):\Pi_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{e}\mapsto-\sum\limits_{x_{e}}\beta_{e}(x_{e})\cdot\log\bigl(\beta_{e}(x_{e})\bigr).

Here,
U_{\mathrm{B}}
is the Bethe average energy function and
H_{\mathrm{B}}
is the Bethe entropy function.
(Note that for NFGs with full and half edges, the sum in
\sum\limits_{e}H_{\mathrm{B},e}(\bm{\beta}_{e})
is only over all full
edges.) The Bethe approximation of the partition function, henceforth
called the Bethe partition function, is defined to be

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle\triangleq\exp\left(-\min_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})}F_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta})\right).

(2.9)

This definition is often viewed as the primal formulation of the Bethe partition function.

\triangle

Proposition 17
.

Consider
\mathsf{N}
to be cycle-free, i.e., tree-structured.

1.

[
YFW05
, Proposition 3]
It holds that
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})=Z(\mathsf{N})
.

2.

(See, e.g.,
[
JI08
, Section 2.5]
.) The SPA on
\mathsf{N}
converges to a unique fixed point and the beliefs evaluated by the SPA fixed-point messages, as defined in Definition
12
, is at the location of the minimum of the Bethe free energy function, i.e., the value of the Bethe partition function can be evaluated by the fixed-point messages.

By these two properties, we know that for a cycle-free S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
,
the value of the partition function can be evaluated by the SPA fixed-point messages.

\square

Remark 18
.

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
with cycles.
Suppose that we get an SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
and obtain a collection of the beliefs
\bm{\beta}
based on
\bm{\mu}
, following Definition
12
.
One can verify that

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N}).

Therefore, the value of the function
\exp\bigl(-F_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta})\bigr)
, which is obtained by
\bm{\mu}
, can be viewed as an approximation of the value of the Bethe partition function.

For an S-NFG with cycles, the behavior of the SPA varies and the Bethe partition function provides an approximation of the partition function. One of the main results in the paper by Yedidia
et al.

[
YFW05
, Section V]
is a characterization of the stationary points of the Bethe free energy function in terms of the SPA fixed-point messages.

Theorem 19
.

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
.

1.

[
YFW05
, Theorem 2]
If the stationary point of the Bethe free energy function is in the interior of the LMP, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N}),\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\beta}_{f}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{|\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}|},\hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F},

then this stationary point corresponds to an SPA fixed point of
\mathsf{N}
.

2.

[
YFW05
, Theorem 3]
If all the local functions in
\mathsf{N}
are positive-valued, i.e.,

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f},\,f\in\mathcal{F},

then each of the local minima of the Bethe free energy function corresponds to an SPA fixed point of
\mathsf{N}
.

\square

For S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
with cycles,
the SPA on
\mathsf{N}
might converge to a fixed point, or it might fail to converge.
The Bethe partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
can be smaller,
equal, or larger than the partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
.

1.

In
[
Ruo12
, Corollary 4.2]
, Ruozzi proved that the Bethe partition function lower bounds the partition function for S-NFGs whose global functions are a binary and log-supermodular function. In subsequent work
[
Ruo13
]
, Ruozzi extended this lower bound to certain classes of S-NFGs whose global functions are not necessarily binary or log-supermodular, including S-NFGs for ferromagnetic Potts models with a uniform external field
[
Ruo13
, Theorems 5.2 and 5.4]
and its generalizations
[
Ruo13
, Theorem 5.6]
, as well as a class of S-NFGs related to the problem of counting weighted graph homomorphisms.
[
Ruo13
, Theorem 6.2]
.

2.

In
[
Von13a
]
, Vontobel investigated the S-NFG whose partition function equals the permanent of a non-negative square matrix. For such S-NFG, he showed that the Bethe free energy function is a convex function and the SPA finds the minimum of the Bethe free energy function exponentially fast. Huang and Vontobel
[
HV24
]
extended Vontobel’s results to a broader class of bipartite S-FGs where each local function is defined based on a (possibly different) multi-affine homogeneous real stable (MAHRS) polynomial. They further showed that a certain projection of the local marginal polytope equals the convex hull of the set of valid configurations, the Bethe free energy function possesses some convexity properties, and, for the typical case where the S-FG has an SPA fixed point consisting of positive-valued messages only, the SPA finds the value of the Bethe partition function exponentially fast.

3.

Gurvits in
[
Gur11
, Theorem 2.2]
proved that for the S-NFG studied in
[
Von13a
]
, the Bethe partition function lower bounds the partition function,

i.e.
, he proved the lower bound in (
1.2
). Subsequent work by Straszak and Vishnoi
[
SV19
, Theorem 3.2]
and Anari and Gharan
[
AG21
, Theorem 1.1]
extended this lower bound to the S-NFGs where each local function corresponds to a (possibly different) multi-affine real stable polynomial. (Note that they did not assume homogenity.)
The class of the S-NFGs considered in
[
SV19
,
AG21
]
is a generalization of the class of the S-NFGs studied in
[
Von13a
,
HV24
]
.

Besides bounding the partition function using the Bethe partition function, we can also connect these two functions via finite graph covers.
Unlike the analytical definition of the Bethe partition function in (
2.9
), Vontobel in
[
Von13
]
provided a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers. A key step in achieving this characterization was to use the method of types to characterize valid configurations in
M
-covers. This characterization is illustrated in Fig.
1
.
For
M
being one, the degree-
M
Bethe partition function is equal to the partition function. As
M
grows to infinity, the limit superior of the degree-
M
Bethe partition function is equal to the Bethe partition function. Section
2.6
provides technical details about
M
-covers and the degree-
M
Bethe partition function.

Given that the Bethe partition function is closely related to the SPA, in the following, we give a pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function based on the SPA fixed-point message vectors.

Definition 20
.

(The pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function for S-NFGs)

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and let

\bm{\mu}
be some SPA
message vector for
\mathsf{N}
as defined in (
2.5
).
4
4

4

The SPA message vector
\bm{\mu}
does
not necessarily have to be an SPA fixed-point message vector.

If

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})>0,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},

(2.10)

then we define the

\bm{\mu}
-based Bethe partition function to be

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\prod_{f}Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})}{\prod\limits_{e}Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})},

(2.11)

where

\displaystyle Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}(x_{e}),\ \hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{x_{e}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}(x_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E}.

(2.12)

(For NFGs with full and half edges, the product in the denominator on the
right-hand side of (
2.11
) is only over all full
edges. Moreover, note that the expression for
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
is scaling
invariant, i.e., if any of the messages
\mu_{e,f}
is multiplied by a
non-zero complex number then
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
remains unchanged.)
The SPA-based Bethe partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
is then defined
to be

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})&\triangleq\max\limits_{\bm{\mu}}Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})\\
&\hskip 23.49976pt\mathrm{s.t.}\ \text{$\bm{\mu}$ is an SPA fixed-point message vector
satisfy~\eqref{eqn:positivity of SNFG Ze}.}\end{aligned}

The above definition is viewed as the pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function.

\triangle

Remark 21
.

Let us make some remarks on the pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function in Definition
20
.

1.

Consider an arbitrary cycle-free S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
.
Proposition
17

states that the SPA on
\mathsf{N}
converges to a unique fixed point
and that
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})=Z(\mathsf{N})
.
Denote the associated SPA fixed-point message vector by
\bm{\mu}
.
One can verify that
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})=Z(\mathsf{N})
.

2.

By Assumption
13
, in this thesis, we only consider the S-NFGs where
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
, as defined in Definition
20
, is well-defined, i.e., the SPA fixed-point messages
\bm{\mu}
such that

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N}),\hskip 23.49976ptZ_{e}(\bm{\mu})>0,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E}.

See Item
3
for a non-trivial S-NFG where this condition is not satisfied.

f_{2}
f_{1}
x_{1}
x_{2}
\latex

@@caption[S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Item

.]S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Item

.
\latex
