.

3.

Let us consider the S-NFG in Fig.
2
, where the dots are used for denoting the row index of the matrices
\bm{f}_{1}
and
\bm{f}_{2}
w.r.t. the function nodes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, respectively. In particular, these matrices are given by

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(f_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\bigr)_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{f}_{2}\triangleq\bigl(f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\bigr)_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}

with row indexed by
x_{1}
and column indexed by
x_{2}
. Running the SPA on this S-NFG is equivalent to applying the power method to the matrix
\bm{f}_{1}\cdot\bm{f}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}
. If we set

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{f}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

then the SPA fixed-point messages are given by

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{1,f_{1}}=\bm{\mu}_{2,f_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}0,&1\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\mathsf{T}},\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\mu}_{2,f_{1}}=\bm{\mu}_{1,f_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}1,&0\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\mathsf{T}},\hskip 11.74988pt

which results in

\displaystyle Z_{1}(\bm{\mu})=Z_{2}(\bm{\mu})=0.

We cannot evaluate
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
for this S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
.

4.

[
YFW05
, Section VI-C]
A sufficient condition for
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
being well-defined for the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
is that the local functions in

\mathsf{N}
satisfy

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})>0,\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f},\,f\in\mathcal{F}.

For such S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
, both the SPA fixed-point messages and the beliefs at the locations of the local minima of the Bethe free energy function are positive-valued.
Thus the function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
is well-defined. The statement proven by Yedidia et al. in
[
YFW05
, Theorem 3]
further implied that

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N}).

5.

Consider running the SPA defined in Definition
11
on some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
.
Suppose that we obtain an SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
. Following Definition
12
, we obtain
the collection of the beliefs
\bm{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{N})
, which is induced by
\bm{\mu}
.
Then we relate the value of the Bethe free energy function evaluated at
\bm{\beta}

to the value of the
\bm{\mu}
-based Bethe partition function by the following equation:

\displaystyle\exp\bigl(-F_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\beta})\bigr)=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu}).

\triangle

2.3
The S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
for the Permanent of a Non-negative Square Matrix
\bm{\theta}

The main purpose of this section is to present an S-NFG whose partition
function equals the permanent of a non-negative matrix
\bm{\theta}
of size

n\times n
. There are different ways to develop such
S-NFGs
[
GJR10
,
Von13a
]
. (For a comparison between the ways
in
[
GJR10
,
Von13a
]
, see
[
Von13a
, Section
VII-E]
.) In this thesis, we consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})

defined in
[
Von13a
]
. Throughout this section, let
\bm{\theta}
be a
fixed non-negative matrix of size
n\times n
.

Definition 22
.

We define the S-NFG

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\triangleq\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
as follows (see
also Fig.
2.3
for the special case
n=3
). Note that
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is a special case of the S-NFG defined in Definition
8
.

1.

The set of vertices (also called function nodes) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{f_{\mathrm{r},i}\}_{i\in[n]}\ \cup\ \{f_{\mathrm{c},j}\}_{j\in[n]}.

Here, the letter “
\mathrm{r}
” in
f_{\mathrm{r},i}
means that
f_{\mathrm{r},i}

corresponds to the
i
-th row of
\bm{\theta}
, whereas the letter
“
\mathrm{c}
” in
f_{\mathrm{c},j}
means that
f_{\mathrm{c},j}
corresponds to the

j
-th column of
\bm{\theta}
.

2.

The set of edges is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\triangleq[n]\times[n]=\bigl\{(i,j)\bigm|i,j\in[n]\bigr\}.

3.

The alphabet associated with edge
e=(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{e}=\mathcal{X}_{i,j}\triangleq\{0,1\}.

4.

The set
\mathcal{X}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\triangleq\prod\limits_{e}\mathcal{X}_{e}=\prod\limits_{i,j}\mathcal{X}_{i,j}

and is called the configuration set of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
.

5.

A matrix like

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(\gamma(i,j)\bigr)_{\!(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]}\in\mathcal{X}

is used to denote an element of
\mathcal{X}
, i.e., a configuration of

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. Here,
\gamma(i,j)
is associated with edge

e=(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}
. In the following, the
i
-th row and the

j
-th column of
\bm{\gamma}
will be denoted by
\bm{\gamma}(i,:)
and

\bm{\gamma}(:,j)
, respectively.

6.

For a given configuration
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{X}
, the value of the local
functions of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
are defined as follows. Namely, for every

i\in[n]
, the local function
f_{\mathrm{r},i}
is defined to
be
5
5

5

Note that, with a slight abuse of notation, we use the same
label for a function node and its associated local function.

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{r},i}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}(i,:)\bigr)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\sqrt{\theta(i,j)}&\bm{\gamma}(i,:)=\bm{u}_{j}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

For every
j\in[n]
, the local function
f_{\mathrm{c},j}
is defined to be

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{c},j}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}(:,j)\bigr)\triangleq\begin{cases}\sqrt{\theta(i,j)}&\bm{\gamma}(:,j)=\bm{u}_{i}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Here we used the following notation: the vector
\bm{u}_{j}
stands for a row
vector of length
n
whose entries are all equal to
0
except for the

j
-th entry, which is equal to
1
. The column vector
\bm{u}_{i}
is defined
similarly.

7.

For a given configuration
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{X}
, the value of the global
function
g
of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is defined to be the product of the values
of the local functions, i.e.,

\displaystyle g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq\Biggl(\prod\limits_{i}f_{\mathrm{r},i}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}(i,:)\bigr)\Biggr)\cdot\Biggl(\prod\limits_{j}f_{\mathrm{c},j}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}(:,j)\bigr)\Biggr).

8.

The set of valid configurations of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is
defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{A}\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{X}\bigm|g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})>0\bigr\}.

9.

The partition function of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is defined to be

\displaystyle Z\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})=\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma}).

\triangle

f_{\mathrm{r},1}
f_{\mathrm{c},1}
f_{\mathrm{r},2}
f_{\mathrm{c},2}
f_{\mathrm{r},3}
f_{\mathrm{c},3}
\latex

@@caption[The S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
for the special case
n=3
.]The S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
for the special case
n=3
.
\latex
@@caption[The S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
for the special case
n=3
.]The S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
for the special case
n=3
.

We make the following observations:

1.

One can verify that
Z\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)=\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
.

2.

If
g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})>0
, then
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
,

i.e.
,
\bm{\gamma}
is a permutation matrix of size
n\times n
. (In
fact, if all entries of
\bm{\theta}
are strictly positive, then

g_{\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})>0
if and only if
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
.)

For the following definition, recall that
\Gamma_{n}
is the set of all
doubly stochastic matrices of size
n\times n
and that
\Gamma_{M,n}
is the
set of all doubly stochastic matrices of size
n\times n
where all entries
are multiples of
1/M
.

For the following definition, recall that
\Gamma_{n}
is the set of all
doubly stochastic matrices of size
n\times n
and that
\Gamma_{M,n}
is the
set of all doubly stochastic matrices of size
n\times n
where all entries
are multiples of
1/M
.

Definition 23
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. We make the
following definitions.

1.

We define
\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
to be the set of matrices in
\Gamma_{n}
whose
support is contained in the support of
\bm{\theta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}\bigm|\gamma(i,j)=0\text{ if }\theta(i,j)=0\bigr\}.

2.

We define
\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
to be the set of matrices in
\Gamma_{M,n}

whose support is contained in the support of
\bm{\theta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}\bigm|\gamma(i,j)=0\text{ if }\theta(i,j)=0\bigr\}.

3.

We define
\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}
to be the set of vectors representing
probability mass functions over
\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
whose support is contained
in
\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}\triangleq\left\{\bm{p}=\bigl(p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\bigr)_{\!\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}}\ \middle|\!\begin{array}[]{c}p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\geq 0,\,\forall\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}\\
p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})=0,\,\forall\bm{P}_{\sigma}\notin\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})\\
\sum\limits_{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}}p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})=1\end{array}\!\right\}.

4.

Let
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}
. We define

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}\ \middle|\ \sum\limits_{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}}p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\cdot\bm{P}_{\sigma}=\bm{\gamma}\right\}.

5.

For any matrix
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\geq 0}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{B})

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\bigm|B\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)>0,\,\forall i\in[n]\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\ \middle|\ \prod_{i}B\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)>0\right\}.

6.

We define

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}

\displaystyle\triangleq(\sigma_{m})_{m\in[M]}\in(\mathcal{S}_{[n]})^{M},

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}

\displaystyle\triangleq(\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}})_{m\in[M]}\in(\mathcal{P}_{[n]})^{M}.

7.

Let
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. We define

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\prod\limits_{i,j}\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr)^{\!M\cdot\gamma(i,j)}.

8.

Let
p_{\bm{\theta}}
be the probability mass function on
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}

induced by
\bm{\theta}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[n]}\theta\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)}{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}=\frac{\bm{\theta}^{\bm{P}_{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})},\hskip 11.74988pt\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}.

Clearly,
p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma)\geq 0
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
and

\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma)=1
.

\triangle

Note that if
\bm{B}
is a doubly stochastic matrix of size
n\times n
, then
the set
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{B})
is non-empty. Indeed, this follows from
considering

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{B})=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{B})}\prod\limits_{i}B\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\overset{(a)}{\geq}n!/n^{n}>0,

where step
(a)
follows from van der Waerden’s inequality, which was
proven in
[
Ego81
, Theorem 1]
and
[
Fal81
, Theorem
1]
.

Lemma 24
.

The set of valid configurations
\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})
of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})

satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})=\left\{\left.\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}\ \right|\ \sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\theta})\right\},

which implies a bijection between
\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})
and

\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\theta})
.

Proof.

This result follows from the Observation 2 after
Definition
22
.
∎

2.4
Free Energy Functions Associated with

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})

We introduce various free energy functions associated with

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. First we introduce the Gibbs free energy function, whose
minimum yields
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
. Afterwards, we introduce the Bethe free
energy function and the scaled Sinkhorn free energy function, which are
approximations of the Gibbs free energy functions. Their minima yield the
Bethe permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
and the scaled Sinkhorn permanent

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
, respectively, which are approximations of

\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})
. Finally, we introduce the degree-
M
Bethe permanent

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and the degree-
M
scaled Sinkhorn permanent

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
.

Definition 25
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. The Gibbs free energy function
associated with
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
(see
[
YFW05
, Section III]
) is
defined to be

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}:\ \Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\bm{p}

\displaystyle\mapsto U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{p})-H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})

with

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}}p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\cdot\log\bigl(g(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}}p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\cdot\log\bigl(p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\bigr),

where
U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}
and
H_{\mathrm{G}}
are called the Gibbs average
energy function and the Gibbs entropy function, respectively.
6
6

6

Note
that
\mathcal{P}_{[n]}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, where the configuration set

\mathcal{X}
of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
was defined in Definition
22
. With this,
\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
is a
configuration of
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
and
g(\bm{P}_{\sigma})
is well defined.

\triangle

One can verify that

\displaystyle Z\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(-\min_{\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}}F_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{p})\biggr).

Recall the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem (see,
e.g.
,
[
HJ12
, Theorem
4.3.49]
), which states that the convex hull of all permutation
matrices of size
n\times n
equals the set of all doubly stochastic matrices
of size
n\times n
. Because
\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}
is a probability mass
function over the set of all permutation matrices of size
n\times n
with support contained in the support of
\bm{\theta}
, the
Gibbs free energy function can be reformulated as follows as a function over

\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
instead of over
\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}
.

Definition 26
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. The modified Gibbs free energy
function associated with
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is defined to be

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}:\ \Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle\mapsto U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})-H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})

with

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{i,j}\gamma(i,j)\cdot\log\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq\max\limits_{\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p}),

where
U_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}
and
H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
are called the modified Gibbs average energy
function, and the modified Gibbs entropy function, respectively.
7
7

7

Note that the definition

H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})\triangleq\max\limits_{\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})}H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})

would have been more natural/straightforward. However, we prefer the
definition

H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})\triangleq\max\limits_{\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{p})
, as it
does not depend on
\bm{\theta}
and, most importantly is equivalent to the
former definition for
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
. This equivalence
stems from the following observation. Namely, let

\bm{p}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\theta})}(\bm{\gamma})
. Then
p(\bm{P}_{\sigma})>0
only if

\mathrm{supp}(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\bm{\gamma})
.

\triangle

One can verify that

\displaystyle Z\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(-\min_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})}F_{\mathrm{G},\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})\biggr).

However, neither formulation of the Gibbs free energy function leads to a
tractable optimization problem in general unless
n
is small. This motivates
the study of approximations of the permanent based on approximating the Gibbs
free energy function, especially based on approximating the modified Gibbs
free energy function in the reformulation presented in
Definition
26
. In the following, we will first discuss
an approximation known as the Bethe free energy function and then an
approximation known as the scaled Sinkhorn free energy function.

In general, the Bethe free energy function associated with an S-NFG is a
function over the so-called local marginal polytope associated with the
S-NFG. (For a general discussion of the local marginal polytope associated
with an S-NFG, see,
e.g.
,
[
JI08
, Section 4]
.) The thesis

[
Von13a
, Section IV]
shows that the local marginal polytope of the

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
can be parameterized by the set of doubly stochastic
matrices. This is a consequence of the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem.

Lemma 27
.

​​
[
Von13a
, Corollary 15]

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. The Bethe free energy function
associated with
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
, as defined in Definition
16
,
can be reformulated by the mapping
F_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}
, which is defined to be

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}:\ \Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle\mapsto U_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})-H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})

with

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{i,j}\gamma(i,j)\cdot\log\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{i,j}\gamma(i,j)\cdot\log\bigl(\gamma(i,j)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\ +\sum\limits_{i,j}\bigl(1-\gamma(i,j)\bigr)\cdot\log\bigl(1-\gamma(i,j)\bigr).

Here,
U_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}
and
H_{\mathrm{B}}
are called the Bethe average energy function
and the Bethe entropy function, respectively. With this, we have

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta}),

where the Bethe permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\exp\biggl(-\min_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})}F_{\mathrm{B},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})\biggr).

(2.13)

\square

The degree-
M
Bethe permanent of
\bm{\theta}
was defined
in
[
Von13a
]
and yields a combinatorial characterization of the
Bethe permanent. (Note that the definition of
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})

in (
2.13
) is analytical in the sense that
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
is
given by the solution of an optimization problem.)

Definition 28
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The degree-
M

Bethe permanent is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt[M]{\bigl\langle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}})\bigr\rangle_{\bm{P}_{M}\in\tilde{\Psi}_{M}}},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{M}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\bm{P}_{M}\triangleq\Bigl(\bm{P}^{(i,j)}\Bigr)_{\!\!i,j\in[n]}\ \middle|\ \bm{P}^{(i,j)}\in\mathcal{P}_{[M]}\right\},

where
\mathcal{P}_{[M]}
is defined in (
2.1
) and the

\bm{P}_{M}
-lifting of
\bm{\theta}
is defined to be a real-valued matrix of size
Mn
-by-
Mn
:

\displaystyle\!\!\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\theta(1,1)\cdot\bm{P}^{(1,1)}&\cdots&\theta(1,n)\cdot\bm{P}^{(1,n)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\theta(n,1)\cdot\bm{P}^{(n,1)}&\cdots&\theta(n,n)\cdot\bm{P}^{(n,n)}\end{pmatrix}.

\triangle

Proposition 29
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. It holds that

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B},M}\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr),\hskip 23.49976ptM\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}\,\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta}).

(2.14)

The limit in (
2.14
) is visualized in
Fig.
1.1.3
(left).

Proof.

See
[
Von13a
, Section VI]
.
∎

As we will see in Section
2.6
, the degree-
M
Bethe permanent
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
is equal to the degree-
M
Bethe partition function of the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
, and the proof of Proposition
29
is based on using the method of types to characterize the valid configurations in the degree-
M
covers of the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
.

The Sinkhorn free energy function was defined in
[
Von14
]
. Here we
consider a variant called the scaled Sinkhorn free energy function that was
introduced in
[
ACSS21
]
. These free energy functions are also defined
over
\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
.

Definition 30
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. The scaled Sinkhorn free energy
function associated with
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
is defined to be

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{scS},\bm{\theta}}:\ \Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle\mapsto U_{\mathrm{scS},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})-H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})

with

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{scS},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\sum\limits_{i,j}\gamma(i,j)\cdot\log\bigl(\theta(i,j)\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq-\,n-\sum\limits_{i,j}\gamma(i,j)\cdot\log\bigl(\gamma(i,j)\bigr).

Here,
U_{\mathrm{scS},\bm{\theta}}
and
H_{\mathrm{scS}}
are called the scaled Sinkhorn average
energy function and the scaled Sinkhorn entropy function,
respectively. With this, the scaled Sinkhorn permanent is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\exp\biggl(-\min_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})}F_{\mathrm{scS},\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\gamma})\biggr).

(2.15)

\triangle

Note that
H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})
can be obtained from
H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})
by
approximating
\bigl(1-\gamma(i,j)\bigr)\cdot\log( 1 - \gamma(i,j) )
by
the first-order Taylor series expansion term
-\gamma(i,j)
and using the
fact that the sum of all entries of the matrix

\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
equals
n
.

In the following, we introduce the degree-
M
scaled Sinkhorn permanent of

\bm{\theta}
, which yields a combinatorial characterization of the scaled
Sinkhorn permanent of
\bm{\theta}
. (Note that the definition of

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
in (
2.15
) is analytical in the sense
that
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
is given by the solution of an optimization problem.)

Definition 31
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The degree-
M

scaled Sinkhorn permanent is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\bm{\theta}^{\uparrow\bm{P}_{M}}\bigr\rangle_{\bm{P}_{M}\in\tilde{\Psi}_{M}}\Bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt[M]{\mathrm{perm}\bigl(\bm{\theta}\otimes\bm{U}_{M,M}\bigr)},

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product of two matrices and where

\bm{U}_{M,M}
is the matrix of size
M\times M
with all entries equal to

1/M
.
\triangle

Proposition 32
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
. It holds that

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}\,\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta}).

(2.16)

The limit in (
2.16
) is visualized in
Fig.
1.1.3
(right).

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1
. (Note that this proof needs some results from
Lemma
2
and
Proposition
5
, which
are proven only in the upcoming sections. Of course, Lemma
2
and
Proposition
5
are
proven independently of the statement in Proposition
32
.)
∎

Except for small values of
n
, computing
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
appears to be intractable in general for finite

M
.
8
8

8

More formally, we leave it as an open problem to show that
computing
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
is in the
complexity class #P for finite
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
However, as
M
goes to
infinity, the limit superior of
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
are equal to
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})
and

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})
, respectively, and they can be computed
efficiently
[
Von13a
,
LSW00
]
.

Note that the modified Gibbs entropy function
H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})
, the Bethe entropy function
H_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\gamma})
, and the scaled Sinkhorn entropy function
H_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\gamma})
, as defined in Definitions
26
,
27
, and
30
, respectively, are well defined for
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}
, not just for
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
. This observation allows us to consider
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}

in Proposition
5
,
i.e.
,
\bm{\gamma}
is an element of a set that is independent of
\bm{\theta}
.

2.5
Double-Edge Normal Factor Graphs (DE-NFGs)

Now we move to the quantum case. Instead of using the bra-ket notation of quantum mechanics, we will use standard linear algebra notation similar as in
[
LV17
,
LV20
]
.

In this section, we review DE-NFGs, which are based on the works in
[
LV17
,
LV20
,
CV17
]
. In general, a DE-NFG can
contain four types of edges: full double edges, full single edges, half double
edges, and half single edges. For the purposes of this thesis, it is sufficient
to consider only DE-NFGs where all the edges are full double edges. The
reasoning is as follows: similar to Section
2.2
, half
(double/single) edges can be turned into full
(double/single) edges by attaching a dummy
1
-valued function node to every
half (double/single) edge without changing any marginals or the partition
function. Moreover, DE-NFGs with full single edges can be turned into DE-NFGs
with only full double edges by changing full single edges into full double
edges and suitably reformulating function nodes.

We start by presenting an example DE-NFG.

Example 33
.

\tilde{x}_{1}
\tilde{x}_{2}
\tilde{x}_{3}
\tilde{x}_{4}
\tilde{x}_{5}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
\latex

@@caption[DE-NFG in Example
33
.]DE-NFG in Example
33
.
\latex
@@caption[DE-NFG in Example
33
.]DE-NFG in Example
33
.

The DE-NFG in Fig.
33
depicts the following factorization

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{5},x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,

\displaystyle x_{5}^{\prime})\triangleq f_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cdot f_{2}(x_{1},x_{4},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{4}^{\prime})\cdot f_{3}(x_{2},x_{5},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{5}^{\prime})\cdot f_{4}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{3}^{\prime},x_{4}^{\prime},x_{5}^{\prime}).

For each double edge
e\in[5]
, we associate it with the variable

\tilde{x}_{e}=(x_{e},x^{\prime}_{e})
, where
x_{e}
and
x^{\prime}_{e}
take values in the same
alphabet
\mathcal{X}_{e}
. Moreover, if
e
is incident on a function node

f
, then
\tilde{x}_{e}
is an argument of the local function
f
, i.e., both

x_{e}
and
x^{\prime}_{e}
are arguments of
f
.

\square

Definition 34
.

A DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\tilde{\mathcal{X}})
consists of the following
objects:

1.

A graph
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
with vertex set
\mathcal{F}
and edge set

\mathcal{E}
. The set
\mathcal{F}
represents the set of function nodes, while
\mathcal{E}
represents the set of full double edges. (As
mentioned above, every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
will be assumed to be a full
double edge connecting two function nodes.)

2.

An alphabet
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
, where

\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\triangleq\mathcal{X}_{e}\times\mathcal{X}_{e}
is the alphabet of variable

\tilde{x}_{e}\triangleq(x_{e},x_{e}^{\prime})
associated with edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
.
Without loss of generality,
for each
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we suppose that
(0,0)\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.

For a given DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\tilde{\mathcal{X}})
, we fix the order of the function nodes by
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{|\mathcal{F}|}\}.
Then we make the following definitions.

4.

For every function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the set
\partial f
is defined to be
the set of edges incident on
f
.

5.

For every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, the set
\partial e
represents the set
of function nodes that are connected to
e
.

6.

Consider
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
. The alphabets
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}
are defined to be

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}=\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{X}_{e}\times\mathcal{X}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pt\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{X}_{e}.

For any mapping
h:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}\to\mathbb{C}
, we define
the following complex-valued square matrix:

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{h}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(h(\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}},\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime})\bigr)_{\!\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}=(\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}},\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|\times|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|}

(2.17)

with row indices
\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}
and column indices

\bm{x}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

7.

Consider
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
. The sets

\mathrm{Herm}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}}
and

\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}}
are defined to be
the set of Hermitian matrices of size

|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|\times|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|

and
the set of Hermitian, positive semi-definite (PSD) matrices of size

|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|\times|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{I}}|
, respectively.

8.

An assignment
\tilde{\bm{x}}\triangleq(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}

is called a configuration of the DE-NFG. For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, a
configuration
\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
induces the vector

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}=(\tilde{x}_{e})_{e\in\partial f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}.

9.

For each function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
, there is a local function associated with it, with some slight abuse of notation, also called
f
. Depending on the
conditions imposed on
f
, we distinguish between a strict-sense and a
weak-sense DE-NFG.

(a)

In the case of a
strict-sense DE-NFG
, the local function

f
can be an arbitrary mapping from
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}
to
\mathbb{C}
satisfying
the following property: the square matrix

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{f}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}

(2.18)

with row indices
\bm{x}_{\partial f}
and column indices
\bm{x}_{\partial f}^{\prime}
is a complex-valued,
Hermitian, positive semi-definite (PSD) matrix. Motivated by the use of
DE-NFG in quantum information processing (see, e.g.,

[
WBC15
]
), the matrix
\bm{C}_{f}
will be
called the Choi-matrix representation of
f
or the Choi matrix associated
with
f
.

(b)

In the case of a
weak-sense DE-NFG
, we require that

\bm{C}_{f}\in\mathrm{Herm}_{\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}
, i.e., the matrix
\bm{C}_{f}
is a complex-valued, Hermitian matrix. However, we do

not
require
\bm{C}_{f}
to be a PSD matrix.

(c)

For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
in a strict-sense or weak-sense DE-NFG,
the mappings

\displaystyle u_{f,\ell_{f}}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\lambda_{f}:\mathcal{L}_{f}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

where
\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}
,
are defined to be the mappings satisfying the following equalities and inequalities:

\displaystyle f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}}\lambda_{f}(\ell_{f})\cdot u_{f,\ell_{f}}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\overline{u_{f,\ell_{f}}(\bm{x}_{\partial f}^{\prime})},\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f},

(2.19)

\displaystyle[\ell_{f}\!=\!\ell_{f}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}}u_{f,\ell_{f}}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\overline{u_{f,\ell_{f}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}_{\partial f}^{\prime})},\hskip 23.49976pt\ell_{f},\ell_{f}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{f},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\lambda_{f}(0)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{f}(1)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{f}(|\mathcal{L}_{f}|-1),

(2.21)

where
\mathcal{L}_{f}\triangleq\{0,\ldots,|\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}|-1\}

is a finite set with
|\mathcal{L}_{f}|=|\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}|
.
If we define
\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}
to be the column vector associated with
u_{f,\ell_{f}}
as follows

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}\triangleq\bigl(u_{f,\ell_{f}}(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\bigr)_{\!\bm{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}|},

then the conditions in (
2.19
)–(
2.21
) are equivalent to

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}}\lambda_{f}(\ell_{f})\cdot\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}\cdot\bigl(\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}\bigr)^{\!\mathsf{H}},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\bigl(\bm{u}_{f,\ell^{\prime}_{f}}\bigr)^{\!\mathsf{H}}\cdot\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}

\displaystyle=\bigl[\ell^{\prime}_{f}\!=\!\ell_{f}\bigr],\hskip 23.49976pt\ell_{f},\ell^{\prime}_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}.

In summary, the vectors
\bigl(\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}\bigr)_{\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}}
and the coefficients
\bigl(\lambda_{f}(\ell_{f})\bigr)_{\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}}
form an eigenvalue decomposition of the Hermitian matrix
\bm{C}_{f}
, where
\lambda_{f}(\ell_{f})
is the eigenvalue associated with the right-eigenvector

\bm{u}_{f,\ell_{f}}
.

10.

The global function
g
is defined to be the mapping

\displaystyle g:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{C},\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{\bm{x}}\mapsto\prod\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr).

11.

A configuration
\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
satisfying
g(\tilde{\bm{x}})\neq 0
is
called a valid configuration.

12.

The partition function is defined to be

\displaystyle Z(\mathsf{N})\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}g(\tilde{\bm{x}}).

For a strict-sense
DE-NFG, the partition function satisfies
Z(\mathsf{N})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, whereas
for a weak-sense DE-NFG, the partition function satisfies

Z(\mathsf{N})\in\mathbb{R}
(see
Appendix
A.1
).

\triangle

If there is no ambiguity, when we consider S-NFG, we will use the short-hands

\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}
,

\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}
,

\sum\limits_{e}
,

\sum\limits_{f}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}
,

\prod\limits_{e}
,

\prod\limits_{f}
,

(\cdot)_{\tilde{x}_{e}}
,
and

(\cdot)_{e}
for

\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}}
,

\sum\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,

\sum\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}}
,

\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,

\prod\limits_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
,

(\cdot)_{\tilde{x}_{e}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}}
,
and

(\cdot)_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
,
respectively.
For any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, we will use the short-hands

\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}
,
\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}
,
\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,

(\cdot)_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
and
\max\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
for

\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\prod\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,

(\cdot)_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
and
\max\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
,
respectively.
Moreover,
\partial f\setminus e
will be short-hand notation for

\partial f\setminus\{e\}
.

Assumption 35
.

In the following, unless stated otherwise, when referring to a DE-NFG, we mean a strict-sense DE-NFG, i.e., we consider
\lambda_{f}(\ell_{f})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
\ell_{f}\in\mathcal{L}_{f}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

Furthermore, we will only consider the (strict-sense) DE-NFG

\mathsf{N}
for which
Z(\mathsf{N})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
9
9

9

In this thesis, we only consider the NFGs whose pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function is well-defined. (See the detailed discussion for S-NFG and DE-NFGs in
Remark
21
and Assumptions
35
and
42
.)

\square

One of the motivations for considering DE-NFGs is that many NFGs
with complex-valued local functions in quantum information processing
can be transformed into DE-NFGs. (See
[
CV17
]
for further examples and details on this transformation.)

Example 36

(
[
LV12
,
LV17
]
)
.

Let us consider an NFG in Fig.
2.5
(left). Note that at the end of each edge there is a dot used for denoting the row index of the matrix w.r.t. the local function. In particular, it describes a quantum system with two consecutive unitary evolutions:

•

At the first stage, a quantum mechanical system is
prepared in a mixed state represented by a complex-valued PSD density matrix:

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}\triangleq\bigl(\rho(x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})\bigr)_{\!x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}

with trace one, where the row is indexed by
x_{1}
and the column indexed by
x_{1}^{\prime}
.

•

Then the system experiences two consecutive unitary
evolutions, which are represented by the pair of function nodes

U
and
B
, respectively. The associated matrices
\bigl(U(x_{2},x_{1})\bigr)_{\!x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2},x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}
with row indices
x_{2}
and column indices
x_{1}
and
\bigl(B(x_{3},x_{2})\bigr)_{\!x_{3}\in\mathcal{X}_{3},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}
with row indices
x_{3}
and column indices
x_{2}
are unitary.

In order to transform this NFG into a DE-NFG, certain modifications are made:

•

The variables
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}

and
x_{e}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
are merged into the variable

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{e}=(x_{e},x_{e}^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e},\hskip 23.49976pte\in[3].

•

New local functions are defined based on the rearranged variables.

The resulting DE-NFG is shown in Fig.
2.5
(right).
As an example, local function
\tilde{U}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\tilde{U}(\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{1})\triangleq U(x_{2},x_{1})\cdot\overline{U(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})},\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{x}_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2},\,\tilde{x}_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}.

One can verify that the associated Choi-matrix representation

\bm{C}_{\tilde{U}}
is a PSD matrix with row indices

(x_{2},x_{1})
and
column indices

(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})
.

\square

Definition 37
.

The SPA for DE-NFG is a straightforward application of the SPA for S-NFG. Consider an arbitrary DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. The details of the SPA for
\mathsf{N}
are presented as follows.

1.

(Initialization) For every
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
f\in\partial e
, the
messages
\mu_{e,f}^{(0)}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\to\mathbb{C}
, are initialized as some
arbitrary function. (A typical choice is

\mu_{e,f}^{(0)}(\tilde{x}_{e})\triangleq|\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}|^{-1}
for
all
\tilde{x}_{e}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.)

2.

(Iteration) For
t=1,2,3,\ldots
, the following calculations are performed until a termination criterion is met.
10
10

10

The termination
criterion is typically a combination of numerical convergence and an
upper bound on the number of iterations.

(a)

For
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
,
we define the scaling factors
\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{C}
to be

\displaystyle\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f_{i}}}f_{i}\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f_{i}}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e^{\prime}\in\partial f_{i}\setminus e}\mu_{e^{\prime},f_{i}}^{(t-1)}(\tilde{x}_{e^{\prime}}).

One can obtain the definition of
\kappa_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}
by switching
f_{i}
with
f_{j}
in the above definition.

(b)

If
\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\kappa_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}\cdot\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}\bigr)=0
, we generate random values for each element in the message vector
\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}
by sampling from a uniform distribution in the interval
[0,1]
.

(c)

If
\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\kappa_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}\cdot\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}\bigr)\neq 0
, valid message updates are performed.
For each
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
, we define the message from the edge
e
to

f_{j}
via the mapping

\displaystyle\mu_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\tilde{z}_{e}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{e,f_{j}}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\!-1}\cdot\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f_{j}}:\,\tilde{x}_{e}=\tilde{z}_{e}}f_{j}\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f_{j}}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e^{\prime}\in\partial f_{j}\setminus e}\mu_{e^{\prime},f_{j}}^{(t-1)}(\tilde{x}_{e^{\prime}}).

(If desired or necessary, other generalization procedures is allowed.)

One can obtain the definition of
\mu_{e,f_{i}}^{(t)}
by switching
f_{i}
with
f_{j}
in the above definition.

(d)

For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we define

\displaystyle\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}}f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e}).

If
\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\neq 0
, then we define the belief function
at the function node
f
to be the mapping

\displaystyle\beta_{f}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{f}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\!-1}\cdot f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e}).

(2.23)

(e)

For every
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we define

\displaystyle\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e}).

If
\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\neq 0
, then we define the belief function at edge
e
to be the mapping

\displaystyle\beta_{e}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{e}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\kappa_{e}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\!-1}\cdot\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e}).

(2.24)

3.

At iteration
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the SPA messages can be collected as the SPA message vector
\bm{\mu}^{(t)}
:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{(t)}\triangleq\Bigl(\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}\Bigr)_{\!e\in\partial f,\,f\in\mathcal{F}},

where

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}\triangleq\Bigl(\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e})\Bigr)_{\!\tilde{x}_{e}}.

With this, the SPA message update rules at Step
2
can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{(t)}=f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(t-1)}\bigr)

for some suitably defined function
f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}
.

4.

An SPA message vector

\bm{\mu}\triangleq(\bm{\mu}_{e,f})_{e\in\partial f,f\in\mathcal{F}}
with

\bm{\mu}_{e,f}\triangleq\bigl(\mu_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e})\bigr)_{\!\tilde{x}_{e}}

is called an SPA fixed-point message vector if

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}=f_{\mathrm{SPA},\mathsf{N}}(\bm{\mu}).

(2.25)

The definitions of the belief functions
\beta_{f}
and
\beta_{e}

evaluated by the SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}

are defined similarly as the definitions in (
2.23
)
and (
2.24
), where we need to replace

\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}
with
\bm{\mu}_{e,f}
for all

e\in\partial f
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

(If desired or necessary, other normalization procedures for messages and
beliefs can be specified. Note that other normalization procedures for beliefs do not change the results in this thesis.)

\triangle

Assumption 38
.

In the context of DE-NFG, if not mentioned otherwise, we will only consider SPA
message vectors
\bm{\mu}^{(t)}
for
which

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{e,f}^{(t)}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{e}},\hskip 23.49976pt

for all
e\in\partial f
,

f\in\mathcal{F}
.
Furthermore, we consider the following normalization procedure for the SPA message vectors
\bm{\mu}^{(t)}
and the collection of the beliefs
\bm{\beta}^{(t)}
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}(\tilde{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=1,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\partial f,\,f\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}\beta^{(t)}_{e}(\tilde{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=1,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}}\beta^{(t)}_{f}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})

\displaystyle=1,\hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F}.

Furthermore, we only consider the SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
such that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}\prod\limits_{f\in\partial e}\mu_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e})\neq 0,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E}.

(2.26)

\square

Lemma 39
.

Consider running the SPA defined in Definition
37
on a DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. The resulting belief functions
\beta_{f}^{(t)}
and
\beta_{e}^{(t)}
for
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
e\in\mathcal{E}
have the following properties.

1.

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have

C_{\mu_{e,f}^{(t)}}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}
for all
e\in\partial f
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
, which further implies

\bm{C}_{\beta_{f}^{(t)}}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and

\bm{C}_{\beta_{e}^{(t)}}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

2.

For any SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
, it holds that
\bm{C}_{\mu_{e,f}}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}
for all
e\in\partial f
and
f\in\mathcal{F}

3.

The beliefs induced by an SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
satisfy the following local consistency properties:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{z}}_{\partial f}:\,\tilde{z}_{e}=\tilde{x}_{e}}\beta_{f}(\tilde{\bm{z}}_{\partial f})=\beta_{e}(\tilde{x}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{x}_{e}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e},\,f\in\partial e,\,e\in\mathcal{E}.

Proof.

The proof for each statement in the lemma is listed as follows.

1.

The statement in Item
1
is a consequence of Assumption
38
and a variant of Schur’s product theorem, which states that the entry-wise
product of two PSD matrices is again a PSD
matrix.
11
11

11

Schur’s product theorem states that the entry-wise product
of two positive definite matrices is a positive definite matrix. Note that
the entry-wise product of two matrices is also known as the Hadamard
product.

2.

The statement in Item
2
is a straightforward result of Item
1
.

3.

The statement in Item
3
is a consequence of the SPA message update rules.

∎

The following definition generalizes Definition
20

from S-NFGs to DE-NFGs.

Definition 40
.

(The pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function for DE-NFGs)

Consider a DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and let

\bm{\mu}
be an SPA
message vector for
\mathsf{N}
, as defined in (
2.25
).
12
12

12

The SPA message vector
\bm{\mu}
does
not necessarily have to be an SPA fixed-point message vector.
Define the following quantities:

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{x}_{e}}\mu_{e,f_{i}}(\tilde{x}_{e})\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(\tilde{x}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E}.

If

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})\neq 0,\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},

(2.27)

then we define the
\bm{\mu}
-based Bethe partition function to be

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\prod\limits_{f}Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})}{\prod\limits_{e}Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})},

(2.28)

where

\displaystyle Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}}f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F}.

(For DE-NFGs with full and half edges, the product in the denominator on the
right-hand side of (
2.28
) is only over all full
edges. Note that the expression for
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
is scaling
invariant, meaning that if any of the messages in
\bm{\mu}
is multiplied by a
non-zero complex number, then
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
remains unchanged.)
(As we will see in the following lemma, the function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
is non-negative real-valued for any SPA message vector
\bm{\mu}
.)
The SPA-based Bethe partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
is then defined
to be

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})&\triangleq\max\limits_{\bm{\mu}}Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})\\
&\hskip 23.49976pt\mathrm{s.t.}\ \text{$\bm{\mu}$ is an SPA fixed-point message vector
satisfy~\eqref{eqn:positivity of Ze}}.\end{aligned}

\triangle

Lemma 41
.

Consider a DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and an SPA message vector
\bm{\mu}
. It holds that

\displaystyle Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\hskip 23.49976ptf\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E}.

Proof.

By Lemma
39
, we know that
\bm{\mu}_{e,f}\in\mathrm{PSD}_{\mathcal{X}_{e}}
for all
e\in\partial f
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Then we
can use Schur’s product theorem to show that
the following matrix with row indices
\bm{x}_{\partial f}
and column indices
\bm{x}_{\partial f}^{\prime}
is a PSD matrix:

\displaystyle\Biggl(f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e})\Biggr)_{\!\!\bm{x}_{\partial f},\bm{x}_{\partial f}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle Z_{f}(\bm{\mu})=\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}}f\bigl(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\bigr)\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mu_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

The proof of
Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
is similar and thus it is omitted here.
∎

Assumption 42
.

In Assumption
38
, we assume that the inequalities in (
2.26
) hold.
We further assume that
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
is well-defined, i.e., there exists an SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
that satisfies

\displaystyle Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N}).

\triangle

Proposition 43
.

A sufficient condition for
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for all SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
is that
for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
in
\mathsf{N}
,
the associated Choi-matrix representation
\bm{C}_{f}
is a positive definite matrix.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.2
.
∎

Proposition 44
.

For any cycle-free DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
,
the SPA has a unique fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
,
and the SPA-based Bethe partition function
gives the exact value of the partition function,
i.e.,

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})=Z(\mathsf{N}).

Proof.

The proof is straightforward and thus is omitted here.
∎

Although
formal

Definitions
15
and
16
could be potentially extended from S-NFGs to DE-NFGs, there is a challenge in dealing with the multi-valued nature of the complex logarithm function. We leave this issue for
future research.

In conclusion, this section provides interesting insights and remarks regarding the DE-NFGs and their associated SPA. In the following, we summarize these points:

1.

For each DE-NFG, the SPA fixed point messages are utilized to define the Bethe partition function, which serves as an approximation of the partition function.

2.

For any strict-sense DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
, both the partition function
Z(\mathsf{N})
and the Bethe partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
are non-negative real-valued functions.

3.

Each S-NFG can be viewed as a special case of a DE-NFG. This observation indicates that the concepts, techniques, and theoretical results that are developed for DE-NFGs, can be applied to S-NFGs as well.

2.6
Finite Graph Covers

This section reviews the concept of finite graph covers of a factor graph in general
[
HA96
,
KLVW07
,
Von13
]
.

Definition 45
.

A graph
\hat{\mathsf{G}}=(\hat{\mathcal{V}},\hat{\mathcal{E}})
with vertex set

\hat{\mathcal{V}}
and edge set
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is the cover of a graph

\mathsf{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
if there exists a graph homomorphism

\pi:\hat{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}
such that for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
and

\hat{v}\in\pi^{-1}(v)
, where
\pi^{-1}
is the pre-image of
v
under
the mapping
\pi
, the neighborhood of
\hat{v}
, i.e.,
the set of all vertices in
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
adjacent to
\hat{v}
, is
mapped bijectively onto the neighborhood of
v
, i.e., the set of all vertices in
\mathcal{V}
adjacent to
v
. Given a cover

\hat{\mathsf{G}}=(\hat{\mathcal{V}},\hat{\mathcal{E}})
, if there is a positive
integer
M
such that
|\pi^{-1}(v)|=M
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, then

\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is called an
M
-cover.

\triangle

Without loss of generality, we can denote the vertex set of an
M
-cover by

\hat{\mathcal{V}}\triangleq\mathcal{V}\times[M]
and define the mapping

\pi:\hat{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}
to be such that
\pi\bigl((v,m)\bigr)=v
for all

(v,m)\in\hat{\mathcal{V}}
. Consider an edge in the original graph connecting nodes
v
and
v^{\prime}
, and the corresponding edge in the
M
-cover connecting
(v,m)
and
(v^{\prime},m^{\prime})
. It holds that

\displaystyle\Bigl(\pi\bigl((v,m)\bigr),\pi\bigl((v^{\prime},m^{\prime})\bigr)\Bigr)=(v,v^{\prime}).

For every edge
e=(v,v^{\prime})
in the original graph, there exists a permutation
\sigma_{e}\in\mathcal{S}_{[M]}
associated with the corresponding edges in the
M
-cover such that the set of the corresponding edges can be represented by the following set:

\displaystyle\left\{\Bigl((v,m),\bigl(v^{\prime},\sigma_{e}(m)\bigr)\Bigr)\ \middle|\ m\in[M]\right\}.

This observation motivates the following definition.

Definition 46
.

Given an NFG
\mathsf{N}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
, we define

\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}
to be the set of all
M
-covers. Each
M
-cover

\hat{\mathsf{N}}=(\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{E}})
consists of the vertex set

\hat{\mathcal{F}}\triangleq\mathcal{F}\times[M]
and the edge set
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
,
where

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\Bigl(\bigl(f_{i},m_{i}\bigr),\bigl(f_{j},\sigma_{e}(m_{i})\bigr)\Bigr)\ \middle|\ e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E},\,m_{i}\in[M]\right\}

for some collection of permutations

(\sigma_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\in\mathcal{S}_{[M]}^{|\mathcal{E}|}
.

\triangle

For a given graph
\mathsf{G}
, we consider all graphs in
\mathcal{G}_{M}
to be
distinct graphs, despite the fact that some graphs in the set
\mathcal{G}_{M}

might be isomorphic to each other. (See also the comments on labeled graph
covers after
[
Von13
, Definition 19]
.)

Let
\mathsf{N}
be some S-NFG or DE-NFG. It is straightforward to
define the set
\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}
of
M
-covers
\hat{\mathsf{N}}
of
\mathsf{N}
. (For a
technical description, see
[
Von13
]
.) More importantly, for finite graph covers of DE-NFGs, the double edges are permuted together
13
13

13

A graph-cover definition based on splitting the two edges comprising a double edge could be considered. However, the resulting graph covers would not be immediately relevant for analyzing the SPA in the way that it is formulated. We leave it for future research.

of

.]Left: NFG

\mathsf{\hat{N}}
of

.

\mathsf{N}

edges.

\mathsf{N}

double edges.
The possible

\mathsf{N}
are

M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

M

Definition 48
.

Let
\mathsf{N}
be an S-NFG or a DE-NFG. For any positive integer
M
, we
define the degree-
M
Bethe partition function of
\mathsf{N}
to be

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\triangleq\sqrt[M]{\Bigl\langle Z\bigl(\hat{\mathsf{N}}\bigr)\Bigr\rangle_{\!\!\hat{\mathsf{N}}\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}}}\ ,

where

\displaystyle\bigl\langle Z\bigl(\hat{\mathsf{N}}\bigr)\bigr\rangle_{\hat{\mathsf{N}}\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}}\ \triangleq\frac{1}{|\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}|}\cdot\sum\limits_{\hat{\mathsf{N}}\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}}Z\bigl(\hat{\mathsf{N}}\bigr)

represents the average (more precisely, the arithmetic mean) of

Z\bigl(\hat{\mathsf{N}}\bigr)
over all
\hat{\mathsf{N}}\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}
,
where

\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{M}
is the set of all
M
-covers of
\mathsf{N}
(see
Definition
46
).

\triangle

\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 11.74988pt\left.Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\right|_{M=1}=Z(\mathsf{N})
\hskip 14.22636ptZ_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})
\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\left.Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\right|_{M\to\infty}=Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
\latex

@@caption[A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-NFG.
]A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-NFG.

\latex
@@caption[A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function.]A combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function for an S-NFG.

Theorem 49

(​​
[
Von13
]
)
.

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. It holds that

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N}).

This combinatoral characterization is visualized in Fig.
2.6
.

\square

Remark 50
.

Consider the S-NFG
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
defined in Definition
22
.
Vontobel in
[
Von13a
, Definition 38]
showed that

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B},M}\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr),

where the degree-
M
Bethe permanent is defined in Definition
28
.
Also the limit (
2.14
) in Theorem
49
is equivalent to the limit proven in Proposition
29
in this case.

Given the fact that DE-NFGs are in many ways a natural generalization of
S-NFGs, one wonders if there is a characterization of the Bethe partition
function of a DE-NFG in terms of the partition functions of its graph covers
analogous to Theorem
49
, which motivates the following conjecture.
Note that the right-hand side of the expression in the following conjecture
features
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
, not
Z_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{N})
, because only the
former is defined for DE-NFGs.

Conjecture 51
.

Consider some DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
. It holds that

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N}).

(2.29)

\square

As already mentioned in the introduction, the proof of
Theorem
49
heavily relies on
the method of types and the primal formulation of the Bethe partition function. However, the method of types does not seem to be useful
for proving Conjecture
51
, and it is unclear how to generalize the primal formulation of the Bethe partition function,
i.e.
, Definition
16
, to the case of DE-NFGs, so novel techniques have to be developed. In Theorem
91
in Section
6
, we prove
Conjecture
51
for a class of DE-NFGs
satisfying an easily checkable condition. We conjecture that it holds for more general DE-NFGs.

In the following, we provide promising numerical results supporting
Conjecture
51
.

Example 52
.

Consider the DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
in Fig.
2.6
, where

\mathcal{X}_{e}=\{0,1\}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}=\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)\}

for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Here,
\mathsf{N}
is a
randomly generated DE-NFG, where the local functions for function nodes are independently
generated.

For each instance of
\mathsf{N}
, we run the SPA until it reaches
a fixed point and compute
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
. (We assume that

Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
, where
\bm{\mu}
is the
obtained SPA fixed-point message vector.) For
each instance of
\mathsf{N}
, we compute
Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})
for
M\in[4]
. The following simulation results are for
3000
randomly
generated instances of
\mathsf{N}
.

Specifically, Fig.
2.6
shows the cumulative distribution
function (CDF) of

\bigl(Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\!-\!Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})\bigr)/Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
for
M\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. (Note that

Z_{\mathrm{B},1}(\mathsf{N})=Z(\mathsf{N})
.)
Fig.
2.6
presents the mean
\mu

and standard deviation
\sigma
of the empirical distribution of

\bigl|Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})\!-\!Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})\bigr|/Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
.

The simulation results indicate that, as
M
increases, the degree-
M
Bethe
partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B},M}(\mathsf{N})
approaches SPA-based Bethe partition function
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}^{*}(\mathsf{N})
.

\square
