Chapter 3
Finite-Graph-Covers-based Bound for the Permanent of a Non-negative Square Matrix

In this chapter, we study the degree-
M
Bethe permanent and degree-
M
Sinkhorn permanent, whose definitions are motivated by the degree-
M
finite graph covers of the associated S-NFG, as defined in Section
2.3
. In particular, in Section
3.1
, we present the recursions of the coefficients in expressions of the degree-
M
Bethe permanent and degree-
M
Sinkhorn permanent. Based on these recursions, in Section
3.2
, we prove the main results in Section
1.1.4
,
i.e.
, we bound the permanent via its degree-
M
Bethe permanent and degree-
M
Sinkhorn permanent.
Then in Section
3.3
, we gives asymptotic expressions for the coefficients in the degree-
M
Bethe permanent and degree-
M
Sinkhorn permanent. Finally, in Section
3.4
, we focus on the case where
M=2
and connects some of the results in this chapter with results in other papers.

3.1
Recursions for the

Coefficients

C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,

C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

In the first part of this section, we prove Lemma
2
. Afterwards, we prove some lemmas that will help us in
the proofs of Theorems
3

and
4
in the next section. These latter
lemmas are recursions (in
M
) on the coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
, and
C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
. Note that these recursions
on the coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,
C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
, and

C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
are somewhat analogous to recursions for multinomial
coefficients, in particular also recursions for binomial coefficients like the
well-known recursion

\displaystyle\binom{M}{k}=\binom{M-1}{k-1}+\binom{M-1}{k}.

(See also the discussion in
Example
62
).

Throughout this section, the positive integer
n
and the matrix

\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times n}
are fixed. Recall the definition of
the notation
\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}
in Definition
23
.

Definition 54
.

Consider
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
.

1.

The coefficient
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
is defined to be the
number of
\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}
in
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}
such that

\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}
decomposes
\bm{\gamma}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}}\Bigl[\bm{\gamma}=\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr],

(3.1)

where

\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\triangleq\frac{1}{M}\cdot\sum\limits_{m\in[M]}\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}
and where the notation

[S]
represents the Iverson bracket, i.e.,
[S]\triangleq 1
if the statement

S
is true and
[S]\triangleq 0
if the statement is false.

2.

The coefficient
C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
is defined to be

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq(M!)^{2n-n^{2}}\cdot\prod\limits_{i,j}\frac{\bigl(M-M\cdot\gamma(i,j)\bigr)!}{\bigl(M\cdot\gamma(i,j)\bigr)!}.

3.

The coefficient
C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
is defined to be

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\triangleq M^{-n\cdot M}\cdot\frac{(M!)^{2n}}{\prod\limits_{i,j}\bigl(M\cdot\gamma(i,j)\bigr)!}.

Note that for
M=1
it holds that

\displaystyle C_{1,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{B},1,n}(\bm{\gamma})=C_{\mathrm{scS},1,n}(\bm{\gamma})=1,\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{1,n}.

\triangle

We are now in a position to prove Lemma
2
in Section
1.1.4
.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

1.

The expression in (
1.5
) follows from

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigl(\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}&=\Biggl(\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\prod\limits_{i\in[n]}\theta\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\Biggr)^{\!\!\!M}\\
&\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma}),\end{aligned}

where step
(a)
follows from expanding the right-hand side of the first
line, along with using the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem and
Eq. (
3.1
).

While it is clear that
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})\geq 0
for all

\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
, note that the first inequality
in (
1.8
) in Theorem
3
(proven later in this thesis) can be used to show that

C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})>0
for all
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
.

2.

Eq. (
1.6
) follows from
[
Von13
, Lemma 29]
,
where the role of the set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{M}
in
[
Von13
, Lemma 29]
is
played by the set
\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
in this thesis.

The set
\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
is defined as follows. Namely, as
mentioned in Section
2.4
, the paper

[
Von13a
, Section IV]
shows that the local marginal polytope of

\mathsf{N}(\bm{\theta})
can be parameterized by the set of doubly stochastic
matrices, more precisely it can be parameterized by

\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
. For arbitrary
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the set

\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})\subseteq\Gamma_{n}(\bm{\theta})
is then defined to
be the parameterization of the set of pseudo-marginal vectors in the local
marginal polytope that are
M
-cover lift-realizable pseudo-marginal
vectors. (For the definition of
M
-cover lift-realizable pseudo-marginal
vectors, see
[
Von13
, Section VI]
.)

We claim
\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})=\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
. Indeed, it
follows from the construction of pseudo-marginal vectors based on

M
-covers that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})\subseteq\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
,
and it follows from the construction in the proof
of
[
Von13
, Lemma 29]
that for every element of

\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
the corresponding pseudo-marginal vector is

M
-cover lift-realizable,
i.e.
,

\Gamma^{\prime}_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})\supseteq\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})
.

3.

Eq. (
1.7
) follows
from
[
Fri79
, Theorem 2.1]
.

\blacksquare

The matrices that are introduced in the following definition will turn out to
be useful to formulate the upcoming results.

Definition 55
.

Consider a matrix
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}
.

1.

We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{R}(\bm{\gamma})\triangleq\bigl\{i\in[n]\bigm|\exists j\in[n],\text{ s.t. }0<\gamma(i,j)<1\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{C}(\bm{\gamma})\triangleq\bigl\{j\in[n]\bigm|\exists i\in[n],\text{ s.t. }0<\gamma(i,j)<1\bigr\},

i.e., the set
\mathcal{R}
consists of the row indices of rows where
\bm{\gamma}

contains at least one fractional entry and, similarly, the set
\mathcal{C}

consists of the column indices of columns where
\bm{\gamma}
contains at least
one fractional entry. Based on
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{C}
, we define

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(\gamma(i,j)\bigr)_{\!(i,j)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}}.

On the other hand, the set
[n]\setminus\mathcal{R}
consists of the row
indices of rows where
\bm{\gamma}
contains exactly one
1
and
0
s
otherwise. Similarly, the set
[n]\setminus\mathcal{C}
consists of the
column indices of columns where
\bm{\gamma}
contains exactly one
1
and
0
s
otherwise.

2.

We define
r(\bm{\gamma})\triangleq|\mathcal{R}|
. One can verify the
following statements:

•

|\mathcal{R}|=|\mathcal{C}|
.

•

Either
r(\bm{\gamma})=0
or
r(\bm{\gamma})\geq 2
, i.e.,

r(\bm{\gamma})=1
is impossible.

•

If
r(\bm{\gamma})=0
, then

\bigl|\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr|=1
.

•

If
r(\bm{\gamma})>0
, then
\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\in\Gamma_{r(\bm{\gamma})}
, i.e., the
matrix
\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
is a doubly stochastic matrix of size

r(\bm{\gamma})\times r(\bm{\gamma})
.

3.

Based on the matrix
\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
, we define the matrix

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(\hat{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\bigr)_{\!(i,j)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}},

where

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\cdot\bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\bigr)}{\Biggl(\prod\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}}\bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\bigr)\Biggr)^{\!\!\!1/r(\bm{\gamma})}}.

Note that the permanent of
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{C}}}\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\cdot\Bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\Bigr)}{\Biggl(\prod\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}}\bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\bigr)\Biggr)^{\!\!\!1/r(\bm{\gamma})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{C}}}\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\cdot\Bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\Bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{R},j\in\mathcal{C}}\bigl(1-\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\bigr)}.

(3.2)

\triangle

Example 56
.

Consider the matrix

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\triangleq\frac{1}{3}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}3&0&0&0\\
0&0&3&0\\
0&1&0&2\\
0&2&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{4}.

We obtain
\mathcal{R}=\{3,4\}
,
\mathcal{C}=\{2,4\}
,
r(\bm{\gamma})=2
,

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
2&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip 23.49976pt\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},

and
\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})=2
.

\square

Definition 57
.

Consider
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
, and

\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
. (The discussion after
Definition
23
ensures that such a
\sigma_{1}
exists.)
We define

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{M-1}\cdot\bigl(M\cdot\bm{\gamma}-\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr).

Note that
\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\in\Gamma_{M-1,n}
.
The matrix

\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}
can be thought of, modulo normalizations, as being
obtained by “peeling off” the permutation matrix
\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}
from

\bm{\gamma}
.

\triangle

Example 58
.

Consider again the matrix
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{4}
that was introduced in
Example
56
. Let
M=3
. Note that

\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{3,4}
. If
\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
is chosen
to be

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(1)=1,\ \ \sigma_{1}(2)=3,\ \ \sigma_{1}(3)=2,\ \ \sigma_{1}(4)=4,

then

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0&0\\
0&0&2&0\\
0&0&0&2\\
0&2&0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{2,4}.

On the other hand, if
\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
is
chosen to be

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(1)=1,\ \ \sigma_{1}(2)=3,\ \ \sigma_{1}(3)=4,\ \ \sigma_{1}(4)=2,

then

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0&0\\
0&0&2&0\\
0&1&0&1\\
0&1&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{2,4}.

\square

The following three lemmas are the key technical results that will allow us to
prove Theorems
3

and
4
in the next section.

Lemma 59
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. It holds that

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}C_{M-1,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr).

Proof.

If
r(\bm{\gamma})=0
, then
\bigl|\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})\bigr|=1
and
the result in the lemma statement is straightforward. If

r(\bm{\gamma})\geq 2
, then the result in the lemma statement follows
from the fact that all permutations in
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
are distinct
and can be used as
\sigma_{1}
in
\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}
for decomposing the
matrix
\bm{\gamma}
.
∎

Lemma 60
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. Based on
\bm{\gamma}
, we
define the set
\mathcal{R}
, the set
\mathcal{C}
, and the matrix
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}

according to Definition
55
. Moreover, for any

\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
, we define
\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}
according
to Definition
57
. It holds
that
1
1

1

Consider
the case where both
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{C}
are non-empty sets. Because each entry
in
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
is positive-valued, we have
\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})>0
, and
so the fraction
1/\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})
is well defined.

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})}\cdot\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}C_{\mathrm{B},M-1,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr),

where we define
\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})\triangleq 1
if
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{C}
are
empty sets.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.2
.
∎

Lemma 61
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. For any

\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})
, we define
\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}
according
to Definition
57
. It holds that
2
2

2

Note
that
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
and so
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})\geq n!/n^{n}
by van
der Waerden’s inequality. With this, the fraction
1/\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})
is
well defined.

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\bigr(\chi(M)\bigl)^{\!n}\,\cdot\,\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})}\cdot\!\!\!\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}\!\!\!C_{\mathrm{scS},M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}),

where

\displaystyle\chi(M)

\displaystyle\triangleq\biggl(\frac{M}{M-1}\biggr)^{\!\!M-1},

which starts at
\bigl.\chi(M)\bigr|_{M=2}\!=\!2

and increases to
\bigl.\chi(M)\bigr|_{M\to\infty}\!=\!\mathrm{e}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.3
.
∎

All objects and quantities in this thesis assume
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Interestingly, it is possible to define these objects and quantities
also for
M=0
, at least formally. However, we will not do this except in
the following example, as it leads to nicer figures (see
Figs.
3.1
–
3.1
).

Example 62
.

The purpose of this example is to visualize the recursions in
Lemmas
59
–
61
for the special case

n=2
, i.e., for matrices
\bm{\gamma}
of size
2\times 2
. For,

k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, define
3
3

3

The case
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
is
special: the matrix
\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}
has to be considered as some formal
matrix and the set
\Gamma_{0,2}
is defined to be the set containing only
the matrix
\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}
.

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{k_{1}+k_{2}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}k_{1}&k_{2}\\
k_{2}&k_{1}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{k_{1}+k_{2},2}.\hskip 11.74988pt

Define

\displaystyle C_{0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\triangleq 1,\ C_{\mathrm{B},0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)\triangleq 1,\ C_{\mathrm{scS},0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)\triangleq 1.

One can the verify that with these definitions, the recursions in
Lemmas
59
–
61
hold also for
M=1
.

With these definitions in place, we can draw diagrams that can be seen as
generalizations of Pascal’s triangle,
4
4

4

These generalizations of
Pascal’s triangle are drawn such that
M
increases from the bottom to the
top. This is different from the typical drawings of Pascal’s triangle
(where
M
increases from the top to the bottom), but matches better the
convention used in Fig.
1.1.3
and
similar figures in
[
Von13
]
(
i.e.
,
[
Von13
, Figs. 7
and 10]
), along with better matching the fact that

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
is based on
liftings
of
\bm{\theta}
.
see
Figs.
3.1
–
3.1
. The meaning of these figures
is as follows. For example, consider the quantity

C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)
. According to
Lemma
59
, this quantity can be obtained via

\displaystyle C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=1\cdot C_{2,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,1)}\bigr)+1\cdot C_{2,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=1\cdot 2+1\cdot 1

\displaystyle=3.

(3.3)

The recursion in (
3.3
) is visualized in
Fig.
3.1
as follows. First, the
vertex/box above the label “
C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)
”
contains the value of
C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)
. Second, the
arrows indicate that
C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)
is obtained by
adding
1
times
C_{2,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,1)}\bigr)
and
1
times

C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,2)}\bigr)
.

Moreover, notice that the value of
C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)

equals the number of directed paths from the vertex labeled
“
C_{0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)
” to the vertex labeled
“
C_{3,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(1,2)}\bigr)
”. In general, for

k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the value of

C_{k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
equals the number of
directed paths from the vertex labeled
“
C_{0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)
” to the vertex labeled
“
C_{k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
”. In this context,
recall from
Proposition
5
that

\displaystyle C_{k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\approx\exp\Bigl((k_{1}+k_{2})\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)\Bigr),

where the approximation is up to
o(k_{1}+k_{2})
in the exponent.

Similar statements can be made for

C_{\mathrm{B},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
and

C_{\mathrm{scS},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
in
Fig.
3.1
and
Fig.
3.1
, respectively. Namely, for

k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the value of

C_{\mathrm{B},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
equals the sum of weighted
directed paths from the vertex labeled
“
C_{\mathrm{B},0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)
” to the vertex labeled
“
C_{\mathrm{B},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
”, and

C_{\mathrm{scS},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
equals the sum of
weighted directed paths from the vertex labeled
“
C_{\mathrm{scS},0,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(0,0)}\bigr)
” to the vertex labeled
“
C_{\mathrm{scS},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)
”. (Here, the weight
of a directed path is given by the product of the weights of the directed
edges in the path.) In this context, recall from
Proposition
5
that

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{B},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\approx\exp\Bigl((k_{1}+k_{2})\cdot H_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)\Bigr),

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{scS},k_{1}+k_{2},n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\approx\exp\Bigl((k_{1}+k_{2})\cdot H_{\mathrm{scS}}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\bigr)\Bigr),

where the approximation is up to
o(k_{1}+k_{2})
in the exponent.

\square

In the context of Example
62
, note
that for
n=2
the matrix

\displaystyle\bm{1}_{2\times 2}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}

achieves the upper bound
2^{n/2}=2

in (
1.2
), and with that the matrix

\bm{1}_{2\times 2}
is the “worst-case matrix” for
n=2
in the sense
that the ratio
\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}=2
for

\bm{\theta}=\bm{1}_{2\times 2}
is the furthest away from the ratio

\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}=1
for diagonal
matrices
\bm{\theta}
. However, this behavior of the all-one matrix for
n=2

is atypical. Namely, letting
\bm{1}_{n\times n}
be the all-one matrix for

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, one obtains, according to

[
Von13a
, Lemma 48]
,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}\right|_{\bm{\theta}=\bm{1}_{n\times n}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi n}{\mathrm{e}}}\cdot\bigl(1+o_{n}(1)\bigr),

i.e.
, the ratio

\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{1}_{n\times n})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{1}_{n\times n})}
is much
closer to
1
than to
2^{n/2}
for large values of
n
. (In the above
expression, the term
o_{n}(\ldots)
is based on the usual little-o notation for
a function in
n
.)

In the case of the scaled Sinkhorn permanent, the “worst-case matrix” is
given by the all-one matrix not just for
n=2
, but for all

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
,
i.e.
, the ratio

\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}=\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot\frac{n!}{n^{n}}
for

\bm{\theta}=\bm{1}_{n\times n}
is the furthest away from the value of the
ratio
\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}=\mathrm{e}^{n}
for diagonal matrices

\bm{\theta}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
.

3.2
Bounding the Permanent of
\bm{\theta}

In this section, we first prove Theorem
3
with the help of Lemmas
60

and
61
, where, in order for Lemmas
60

and
61
to be useful, we need to find lower and upper bounds on

\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})
and on
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})
, respectively. Afterwards, we use
Theorem
3
to prove
Theorem
4
.

3.2.1
Bounding the Ratio
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})

In this subsection, we first prove the inequalities in (
1.8
) in
Theorem
3
that give bounds on the
ratio
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})/C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
. Then, based on these bounds, we prove
the inequalities in (
1.10
) in
Theorem
4
that give bounds on the ratio

\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
.

Lemma 63
.

Consider the setup in Lemma
60
. The matrix

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
satisfies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})\leq 2^{n/2}.

(3.4)

Proof.

It is helpful to first prove that
\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})=1
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\exp\biggl(-\min_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{|\mathcal{R}|}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})}F_{\mathrm{B},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}}(\bm{\gamma})\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\exp\bigl(-F_{\mathrm{B},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}}(\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\exp(0)

\displaystyle=1,

where step
(a)
follows from the definition of the Bethe permanent in
Definition
27
, where step
(b)
follows from the convexity
of the minimization problem

\min_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{|\mathcal{R}|}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})}F_{\mathrm{B},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}}(\bm{\gamma})
(which was proven in
[
Von13a
]
) and from studying the
Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT)
conditions for this minimization problem at
\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}
(the
details are omitted), and where step
(c)
follows from simplifying the
expression for
F_{\mathrm{B},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}}(\bm{\gamma}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})
.

With this, the first inequality in (
3.4
) follows from

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}),

where step
(a)
follows from the first inequality
in (
1.2
), and the second inequality
in (
3.4
) follows from

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\underbrace{2^{|\mathcal{R}|/2}}_{\leq\ 2^{n/2}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})}_{=\ 1}\leq 2^{n/2},

where step
(b)
follows from the second inequality
in (
1.2
).

An alternative proof of the first inequality in (
3.4
) goes
as follows. Namely, this inequality can be obtained by
combining (
3.2
) and

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{C}}}\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{R}}\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\cdot\Bigl(1\!-\!\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}\bigl(i,\sigma(i)\bigr)\Bigr)\overset{(a)}{\geq}\!\!\prod\limits_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}}\!\!\bigl(1\!-\!\gamma_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\bigr),

where step
(a)
follows from
[
Sch98
, Corollary 1c of
Theorem 1]
. Note that this alternative proof for the first
inequality in (
3.4
) is not really that different from the
above proof because the first inequality
in (
1.2
) is also based
on
[
Sch98
, Corollary 1c of Theorem 1]

∎

Proof of the inequalities in (
1.8
) in Theorem
3
.

•

We prove the first inequality in (
1.8
) by induction on

M
. The base case is
M=1
. From Definition
54
, it
follows that
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})=C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})=1
for

\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
and so

\displaystyle\frac{C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\geq 1,\hskip 11.74988pt\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n},

is indeed satisfied for
M=1
.

Consider now an arbitrary
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. By the induction hypothesis,
we have

\displaystyle\frac{C_{M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{B},M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\geq 1,\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M-1,n}.

(3.5)

Then we obtain

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}C_{M-1,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}C_{\mathrm{B},M-1,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathrm{perm}(\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}})\cdot C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma}),

where step
(a)
follows from Lemma
59
, where step

(b)
follows from the induction hypothesis in (
3.5
), where step
(c)
follows from
Lemma
60
, and where step
(d)
follows from
Lemma
63
.

•

A similar line of reasoning can be used to prove the second inequality
in (
1.8
); the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

Proof of the inequalities in (
1.10
) in
Theorem
4
.

•

We prove the first inequality in (
1.10
) by observing that

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M},

where step
(a)
follows from (
1.5
), where step
(b)
follows
from the first inequality in (
1.8
), and where step
(c)

follows from (
1.6
). The desired inequality is then obtained
by taking the
M
-th root on both sides of the expression.

A similar line of reasoning can be used to prove the second inequality
in (
1.10
); the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

The expression in the following lemma can be used to obtain a slightly
alternative proof of the inequalities in (
1.10
) in

. However, this result is also

Lemma 64
.

Let
p_{\bm{\theta}}
be the probability mass function on
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
induced
by
\bm{\theta}
(see Definition
23
). It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle=\left(\!\sum\limits_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}}\left(\prod\limits_{m\in[M]}\!\!p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{m})\right)\!\cdot\!\frac{C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr)}{C_{M,n}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr)}\right)^{\!\!\!-1/M}\!\!.

(3.6)

Note that from the first inequality in (
1.8
)
in Theorem
3
it follows that

C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})\geq C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})>0
for all
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
,
i.e., the ratio appearing in (
3.6
) is well
defined.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.4
.
∎

Based on Lemma
64
, we
give an alternative proof of the inequalities in (
1.10
) in
Theorem
4
in Appendix
B.5
.

Note that the first inequality in (
1.10
) is tight. Indeed,
choosing the matrix
\bm{\theta}
to be diagonal with strictly positive diagonal
entries shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})}=1,\hskip 23.49976ptM\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}=\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})}=1.

On the other hand, the second inequality in (
1.10
) is

not
tight for finite
M\geq 2
. Indeed, for this inequality to be
tight, all the inequalities
in (
B.3
) in Appendix
B.4
need to be tight. In
particular, the inequality in step
(a)
needs to be tight, which means that
for all
\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}
with

\prod\limits_{m\in[M]}p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{m})>0
, it needs to hold that

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\ldots)/C_{M,n}(\ldots)=(2^{n/2})^{-(M-1)}
. However, this is not
the case for
\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}=(\sigma,\ldots,\sigma)
with

\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\theta})
, for which it holds that

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\ldots)/C_{M,n}(\ldots)=1
.

Observe that the non-tightness of the second inequality
in (
1.10
) for finite
M\geq 2
is in contrast to the case

M\to\infty
, where it is known that for

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}=\bm{I}_{(n/2)\times(n/2)}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},

it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta})}=\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})}=2^{n/2}.

(Here,
n
is assumed to be even and

\bm{I}_{(n/2)\times(n/2)}
is defined to be the identity matrix of size

(n/2)\times(n/2)
.)

We also give another alternative proof of the inequalities in (
1.10
) in
Theorem
4
in Appendix
B.6
.
In particular, Lemma
92
proven in Appendix
B.6
proves the
M_{1}=1
case of the following conjecture.

Conjecture 65
.

For any integers
M_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
M_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M_{1}}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M_{1}}\cdot\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M_{2}}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M_{2}}}{\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M_{1}+M_{2}}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M_{1}+M_{2}}}\geq 1.

\square

A useful consequence of Conjecture
65
is that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})=\limsup_{M\to\infty}\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})=\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B}}(\bm{\theta}),

where the first equality follows from Conjecture
65
and Fekete’s lemma
[
Fek23
]
(see,
e.g.
,
[
vLW01
, Lemma 11.6]
), and where the second equality follows from
[
Von13a
, Theorem 39]
.

3.2.2
Bounding the Ratio
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})

In this subsection, we first prove the inequalities in (
1.9
)
in Theorem
3
that give bounds on
the ratio
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})/C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
. Then, based on these
bounds, we prove the inequalities in (
1.11
) in
Theorem
4
that give bounds on the ratio

\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
.

Lemma 66
.

Consider the setup in Lemma
61
. The matrix
\bm{\gamma}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{n!}{n^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})\leq 1.

Proof.

The lower bound follows from van der Waerden’s inequality proven
in
[
Ego81
, Theorem 1]
and
[
Fal81
, Theorem 1]
. The
upper bound follows from

\displaystyle\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\prod\limits_{i}\Biggl(\sum\limits_{j}\gamma(i,j)\Biggr)=\prod\limits_{i}1=1,

where step
(a)
follows from the observation that
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})
contains a
subset of the (non-negative) terms in

\prod\limits_{i}\bigl(\sum\limits_{j}\gamma(i,j)\bigr)
.
∎

Proof of the inequalities in (
1.9
) in Theorem
3
.

•

We prove the first inequality in (
1.9
) by induction on

M
. The base case is
M=1
. From Definition
54
, it
follows that
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})=C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})=1
for all

\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
and so

\displaystyle\frac{C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\geq 1,\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}.

(Note that when
M=1
, then the first inequality in (
1.9
)
simplifies to
1
.)

Consider now an arbitrary
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. By the induction
hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\frac{C_{M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma})}{C_{\mathrm{scS},M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\!\geq\!\Biggl(\!\frac{(M\!-\!1)^{M-1}}{(M\!-\!1)!}\!\Biggr)^{\!\!\!n}\!\!\cdot\!\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-2}\!\!\!\!,\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M-1,n}.

(3.7)

Then we obtain

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}C_{M-1,n}\bigl(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\biggl(\frac{(M\!-\!1)^{M-1}}{(M\!-\!1)!}\biggr)^{\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-2}\!\!\!\!\!\!\cdot\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\gamma})}\!\!C_{\mathrm{scS},M-1,n}(\bm{\gamma}_{\sigma_{1}})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\biggr)^{\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-2}\cdot\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\gamma})\cdot C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}\biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\biggr)^{\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-1}\cdot C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma}),

where step
(a)
follows from Lemma
59
, where step

(b)
follows from the induction hypothesis
in (
3.7
),
where step
(c)
follows from
Lemma
61
, and where step
(d)
follows from
Lemma
66
.

•

A similar line of reasoning can be used to prove the second inequality
in (
1.9
); the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

Proof of the inequalities in (
1.11
) in

4
.

•

We prove the first inequality in (
1.11

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\biggr)^{\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggr)^{\!\!M-1}\!\cdot\!\!\!\!\!\sum\limits_{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}(\bm{\theta})}\bm{\theta}^{M\cdot\bm{\gamma}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\biggl(\frac{M^{M}}{M!}\biggl)^{\!\!n}\cdot\biggl(\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\biggl)^{\!\!M-1}\cdot\bigl(\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})\bigr)^{\!M},

where step
(a)
follows from (
1.5
), where step
(b)
follows
from the first inequality in (
1.9
), and where step
(c)

follows from (
1.7
). The desired inequality is then obtained
by taking the
M
-th root on both sides of the expression.

A similar line of reasoning can be used to prove the second inequality
in (
1.11
); the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

The expression in the following lemma can be used to obtain a slightly
alternative proof of the inequalities in (
1.11
) in

. However, this result is also

Lemma 67
.

Let
p_{\bm{\theta}}
be the probability mass function on
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
induced
by
\bm{\theta}
(see Definition
23
). It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle=\left(\!\sum\limits_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}}\!\Biggl(\prod\limits_{m\in[M]}\!\!\!p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{m})\Biggr)\!\!\cdot\!\frac{C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr)}{C_{M,n}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr)}\!\right)^{\!\!\!-1/M}\!\!.

Proof.

The proof of this statement is analogous to the proof of
Lemma
64
in

B.4
; one simply has to

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},M}(\bm{\theta})
and

\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})
and

, respectively.
∎

Alternative proof of the inequalities in (
1.11
) in
Theorem
4
.

Based on Lemma
67
, an alternative proof of the
inequalities in (
1.11
) can be given that is very similar to the
alternative proof presented
in Appendix
B.5
;
the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

Note that the second inequality in (
1.11
) is tight. Indeed,
choosing the matrix
\bm{\theta}
to be diagonal with strictly positive diagonal
entries shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})}=\frac{M^{n}}{(M!)^{n/M}},\hskip 23.49976ptM\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}=\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})}=\mathrm{e}^{n}.

On the other hand, the first inequality in (
1.11
) is
not

tight for finite
M\geq 2
. (The proof is similar to the proof of the
non-tightness of the second inequality in (
1.10
) for finite

M\geq 2
.) This is in contrast to the case
M\to\infty
, where it is known
that for
\bm{\theta}
being equal to the all-one matrix of size
n\times n
it
holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS}}(\bm{\theta})}=\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{scS},M}(\bm{\theta})}=\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot\frac{n!}{n^{n}}.

3.3
Asymptotic Expressions for the
Coefficients
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,

C_{\mathrm{B},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})
,
C_{\mathrm{scS},M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

In this section, we prove
Proposition
5
. Whereas
Definitions
26
,
27
,
and
30
give an analytical definition of the entropy functions

H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
,
H_{\mathrm{B}}
, and
H_{\mathrm{scS}}
, respectively, the expressions in
Eqs. (
1.12
)–(
1.14
) in
Proposition
5
give a
combinatorial characterization of these entropy functions. The proof of
Proposition
5
is based on
standard results of the method of types and considerations that are similar to
the considerations in
[
Von13
, Sections IV and VII]
.

Definition 68
.

Fix some
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}
.
Recall the definition of the notations

\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}
and
\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}
in
Definition
23
. We define the following objects.

1.

Let
\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}
. The type of

\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\bm{t}_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl(t_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}}(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\bigr)_{\!\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})},

where

\displaystyle t_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}}(\bm{P}_{\sigma})

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{M}\cdot\Bigl|\bigl\{m\in[M]\bigm|\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}=\bm{P}_{\sigma}\bigr\}\Bigr|.

2.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}
be the set of all the possible types that are based on the elements in
\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\bm{t}\triangleq\Bigl(t(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\Bigr)_{\!\!\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{c}\exists\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}\\
\text{s.t.}\ \bm{t}_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}}=\bm{t}\end{array}\right\}.

3.

Let
\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}
. The type class of

\bm{t}
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigl\{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}\bigm|\bm{t}_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}}=\bm{t}\bigr\}.

\triangle

Lemma 69
.

Consider
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{n}
. It holds that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\binom{M+|\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})|-1}{|\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})|-1}\in O\bigl(M^{|\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})|-1}\bigr),

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}|

\displaystyle=\frac{M!}{\prod\limits_{\bm{P}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}\bigl(M\cdot t(\bm{P}_{\sigma})\bigr)!},\hskip 23.49976pt\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}.

Proof.

These are standard combinatorial results and so the details of the
derivation are omitted.
∎

Proof sketch of
Proposition
5
.

•

The expression in (
1.12
) can be proven as
follows. Namely, let
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{M,n}
. For a large integer
M
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}^{M}}\Bigl[\bm{\gamma}=\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\bm{P}_{\bm{\sigma}_{[M]}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}}\Bigl[\bm{\gamma}=\bigl\langle\bm{P}_{\sigma_{m}}\bigr\rangle_{m\in[M]}\Bigr]}_{\triangleq\ (*)}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\cap\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}|\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}|

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\sum\limits_{\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\cap\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{t})+o(M)\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{=}\max\limits_{\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\cap\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{t})+o(M)\bigr),

where step
(a)
follows from the definition of
C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

in (
3.1
), where step
(b)
follows from the definition of

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}
, where
step
(c)
follows from the observation that
(*)
equals

|\mathcal{T}_{\bm{t}}|
if

\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\cap\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})
and equals zero
if
\bm{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}\setminus\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})
,
where step
(d)
follows from
Stirling’s approximation of the factorial function (see,
e.g.
,
[
Rob55
]
),
Lemma
69
, and the definition of

H_{\mathrm{G}}
in Definition
25
, and where step
(e)
follows from
the fact that the size of the set
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}
is polynomial
in
M
(see Lemma
69
) and therefore can be absorbed in the

o(M)
term in the exponent.

In the limit
M\to\infty
, the set
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})^{M}}
is dense
in
\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})
, and so

\displaystyle C_{M,n}(\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\max\limits_{\bm{t}\in\Pi_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{\gamma})}(\bm{\gamma})}\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}(\bm{t})+o(M)\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\exp\bigl(M\cdot H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma})+o(M)\bigr),

where step
(a)
used the definition of
H_{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
in
Definition
26
.

•

For the proof of the expression in (
1.13
),
see
[
Von13
, Section VII]
.

•

A similar line of reasoning as above can be used to prove the expression
in (
1.14
); the details are omitted.

\blacksquare

3.4
Results for the Permanent and the Degree-
2
Bethe Permanent

The upcoming proposition, Proposition
70
, is the
same as
[
NV22
, Proposition 2]
. The purpose of this section is
to rederive this proposition based on some of the results that have been
established so far in this thesis.

Proposition 70
.

Let
p_{\bm{\theta}}
be the probability mass function on
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
induced
by
\bm{\theta}
(see Definition
23
). It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{1})\cdot p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot 2^{-c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}\Biggr)^{\!\!\!-1/2},

(3.8)

where
c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
is the number of cylces of length larger than
one in the cycle notation
expression
5
5

5

Let us recall the cycle notation for permutations with the help of an
example. Namely, consider the permutation
\sigma:[7]\to[7]
defined by

\sigma(1)=1
,
\sigma(2)=2
,
\sigma(3)=4
,
\sigma(4)=3
,

\sigma(5)=6
,
\sigma(6)=7
,
\sigma(7)=5
. In terms of the cycle
notation,
\sigma=(1)(2)(34)(567)
. As can be seen from this expression,
the permutation
\sigma
contains two cycles of length
1
(one cycle
involving only
1
and one cycle involving only
2
), one cycle of length

2
(involving
3
and
4
), and one cycle of length
3
(involving
5
,

6
, and
7
).
of the permutation
{\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}^{-1}}
. Here,

\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}^{-1}
represents the permutation that is obtained
by first applying the inverse of the permutation
\sigma_{2}
and then the
permutation
\sigma_{1}
.

\square

Note that
c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies
6
6

6

While the lower bound on

c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
is immediately clear, the upper bound on

c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
follows from permutations of
[n]
that contain

\lfloor n/2\rfloor
cycles of length
2
.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\leq\Bigl\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\Bigr\rfloor,\hskip 23.49976pt\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}(\bm{\theta}).

These bounds on
c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
yield

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sqrt{2^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\leq 2^{n/4},

(3.9)

where the inequality
(a)
is obtained by lower bounding the expression
in (
3.8
) with the help of

c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\geq 0
and where the inequality
(b)
is obtained by
upper bounding the expression in (
3.8
) with
the help of
c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. Note that the
lower and upper bounds in (
3.9
) on the
ratio
\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})/\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})
match exactly the lower and upper
bounds in (
1.10
) for
M=2
.

Remark 71
.

A variant of Proposition
70
(and with that
of
[
NV22
, Proposition 2]
), appears in
[
Csi17
, Lemma
4.2]
. The setup in
[
Csi17
]
is more special than
that in
[
NV22
]
in the sense that Csikvári
in
[
Csi17
]
considered
\{0,1\}
-valued matrices only, not
arbitrary non-negative square matrices. However, the result in
[
Csi17
]

is more general than the result in
[
NV22
]
in the sense that
Csikvári studied matchings of size
k\in[n]
, and not just matchings
of size
n
(i.e., perfect matchings).
\triangle

Alternative proof Proposition
70
.

Let us now show how the results in this thesis can be used to rederive
Proposition
70
. Namely, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{perm}(\bm{\theta})}{\mathrm{perm}_{\mathrm{B},2}(\bm{\theta})}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left(\!\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{1})\cdot p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot\frac{C_{\mathrm{B},2,n}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\!+\!\bm{P}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigr)\Bigr)}{C_{2,n}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\!+\!\bm{P}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigr)\Bigr)}\right)^{\!\!\!-1/2}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\Biggl(\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{1})\cdot p_{\bm{\theta}}(\sigma_{2})\cdot\frac{1}{2^{c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}}\Biggr)^{\!\!\!-1/2},

which is the same expression as
in (
3.8
). Here, step
(a)
follows from
Lemma
64
for
M=2
and step
(b)
follows from

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{B},2,n}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\!+\!\bm{P}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]},

(3.10)

\displaystyle C_{2,n}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{P}_{\sigma_{1}}\!+\!\bm{P}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=2^{c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})},

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}.

(3.11)

Eqs. (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) are established in
Appendix
B.7
.

\blacksquare

Loop calculus, as defined by Chertkov and Chernyak
[
CC06
,
CC07
]
and further developed by Mori
[
Mor15a
]
, can be
formulated as a certain holographic transform
[
AM11
]
applied
to an S-NFG, and will henceforth be called the loop-calculus transform
(LCT). After applying the LCT to an S-NFG, the partition function equals the sum of the Bethe partition function and some correction terms, where the sum of the correction terms are related to a weighted sum over all generalized loops in the S-NFG.

In this chapter, we introduce the LCT for both S-NFGs and DE-NFGs.
The LCT for DE-NFGs has been discussed by Cao in
[
Cao21
, Theorem 2.21]
. Let us make a comparison between the LCT proposed in
[
Mor15a
,
Cao21
]
and our proposed LCT.

•

Mori in
[
Mor15a
]
considered S-NFGs and defined
the LCT based on the SPA fixed-point message vector that consists of positive-valued entries only. Note that he did not define the LCT for the SPA fixed-point message vector that consists of at least one zero-valued entry.

•

Cao in
[
Cao21
]
considered DE-NFGs and defined the LCT for DE-NFGs based on the SPA fixed-point message vector
where the entries are allowed to take values in the field of complex numbers.
However, he only
stated the constraints that the LCT has to satisfy,
and he did not provide explicit expressions that enable us to applying the LCT
for DE-NFGs.

•

In this section, we explicitly define the LCT for both S-NFGs and DE-NFGs. Our proposed LCT is defined based on the SPA fixed-point message vector whose entries can take values in the field of complex numbers,
i.e.
, the SPA fixed-point message vector is allowed to have zero-valued entries. Our proposed LCT generalizes the results in
[
Mor15a
,
Cao21
]
.

•

In Chapter
6
, we use the LCT for DE-NFGs to prove the graph-cover theorem,
i.e.
, Conjecture
51
for a class of DE-NFGs that satisfy an easily checkable condition.

4.1
LCT for S-NFGs

Before giving the general definition of the LCT for S-NFGs, we consider a
specific S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and assume that a part of it looks as shown in
Fig.
4
. Let

\bm{\mu}
be some SPA
fixed-point message vector for
\mathsf{N}
. The LCT is a sequence of
modifications to the original S-NFG that leave the partition function
unchanged. This is done by applying opening-the-box and closing-the box
operations
[
Loe04
]
as follows.

1.

The S-NFG in Fig.
4
is obtained from the S-NFG
in Fig.
4
by applying suitable opening-the-box
operations
[
Loe04
]
. Importantly, the new function nodes
h_{i}

are based on
\bm{\mu}
and are defined in such a way that the partition function remains unchanged.

For example, for the edge
1
that connects the function nodes
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
, as shown in Fig.
4
, we
introduce the function nodes
h_{3}
and
h_{8}
with the local functions

h_{3}:\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
and

h_{8}:\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
, where

\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\triangleq\mathcal{X}_{1}
. The partition function of the S-NFG remains unchanged because
h_{3}
and
h_{8}
are
defined such that the following equalities hold

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathring{x}_{1}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}h_{3}(x_{1,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{1})\cdot h_{8}(x_{1,f_{j}},\mathring{x}_{1})=[x_{1,f_{i}}\!=\!x_{1,f_{j}}],\hskip 23.49976ptx_{1,f_{i}},x_{1,f_{j}}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}.

2.

The S-NFG in Fig.
4
is essentially the same as
the S-NFG in Fig.
4
.

3.

The S-NFG in Fig.
4
is obtained from the S-NFG
in Fig.
4
by applying suitable closing-the-box
operations
[
Loe04
]
to the S-NFG in
Fig.
4
. Consider the dashed boxes on the left-hand side in Fig.
4
. Its exterior function is defined to be the sum, over the internal variables, of the product of the internal local functions. Replacing this dashed box by a single function node that represents this exterior function is known as closing-the-box operation
[
Loe04
]
.
For example, the local function

\mathring{f}_{i}
with the set of adjacent edges
\partial f_{i}=\{1,2,3\}
is
defined to be

\displaystyle\mathring{f}_{i}(\mathring{x}_{1},\mathring{x}_{2},\mathring{x}_{3})

\displaystyle\triangleq\!\!\!\!\!\sum\limits_{x_{1,f_{i}}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},x_{2,f_{i}}\in\mathcal{X}_{2},x_{3,f_{i}}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!f_{i}(x_{1,f_{i}},x_{2,f_{i}},x_{3,f_{i}})\cdot h_{3}(x_{1,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\cdot h_{2}(x_{2,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{2})\cdot h_{4}(x_{3,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{3}).

The partition function of the S-NFG remains unchanged due to the way that
\mathring{f}
is defined.

Let us now give the general definition of the LCT for an S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
that
we use in this thesis, along with stating its main properties.

Definition 72
.

Consider some S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and let

\bm{\mu}
be some SPA
fixed-point message vector.
In the following, for each
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
, we use the
short-hand notation
Z_{e}
for
Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})
, where
(see (
2.12
))

\displaystyle Z_{e}=\sum\limits_{x_{e}}\mu_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e})\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e}).

(Recall that in Assumption
13
, we assume that
Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})>0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.)
We apply the following steps.

1.

(generalization of the first step above) For every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we do the following.

•

For every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we pick a special element of

\mathcal{X}_{e}
. In order to simplify the notation, and without loss of
generality, this special element will be called
0
for all edges, i.e., we suppose that
0\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
.

•

We define the alphabet

\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\triangleq\mathcal{X}_{e}
.
1
1

1

The set
\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
can be different from
\mathcal{X}_{e}
. Essentially, the only requirement is that

|\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}|=|\mathcal{X}_{e}|
. In this thesis, we use the definition
\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\triangleq\mathcal{X}_{e}
for simplicity.

•

The edge
e
(with associated variable
x_{e}
) connecting
f_{i}
to
f_{j}

is replaced by the following alternating sequence of edges and function
nodes: an edge with associated variable
x_{e,f_{i}}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
, a
function node
M_{e,f_{i}}
, an edge with associated variable

\mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
, a function node
M_{e,f_{j}}
, and an edge
with associated variable
x_{e,f_{j}}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
. Here:

–

The function node
M_{e,f_{i}}
is associated with the local function

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{i}}:\mathcal{X}_{e}\times\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\to\mathbb{R}.

–

The function node
M_{e,f_{j}}
is associated with the local function

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{j}}:\mathcal{X}_{e}\times\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\to\mathbb{R}.

In order to ensure that the partition function of the resulting factor graph equals the
partition function of the original S-NFG, the local functions
M_{e,f_{i}}

and
M_{e,f_{j}}
have to satisfy

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathring{x}_{e}}M_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{e})\cdot M_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e,f_{j}},\mathring{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=[x_{e,f_{i}}\!=\!x_{e,f_{j}}],\hskip 23.49976ptx_{e,f_{i}},x_{e,f_{j}}\in\mathcal{X}_{e},

(4.1)

where
\sum\limits_{\mathring{x}_{e}}
represents
\sum\limits_{\mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}}
for simplicity.
Note that (
4.1
) implies
2
2

2

Let

\bm{M}_{1}
and
\bm{M}_{2}
be square matrices satisfying

\bm{M}_{1}\cdot\bm{M}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{I}
, where
\bm{I}
is
the identity matrix of the corresponding size. From linear algebra it
is well known that this implies

\bm{M}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot\bm{M}_{1}=\bm{I}
. Transposing both
sides, one obtains
\bm{M}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot\bm{M}_{2}=\bm{I}
.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x_{e}}M_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e,f_{i}})\cdot M_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e,f_{j}})

\displaystyle=\bigl[\mathring{x}_{e,f_{i}}\!=\!\mathring{x}_{e,f_{j}}\bigr]\hskip 23.49976pt\mathring{x}_{e,f_{i}},\mathring{x}_{e,f_{j}}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}.

(4.2)

•

Let
\zeta_{e,f_{i}}
and
\zeta_{e,f_{j}}
be some arbitrary non-zero
real-valued constants. At the heart of the LCT are the following definitions for

M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{j}}
:

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e}),\hskip 23.49976ptx_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e},\ \mathring{x}_{e}=0,

(4.3)

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e}),\hskip 23.49976ptx_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e},\ \mathring{x}_{e}=0.

(4.4)

Evaluating (
4.2
) for
\mathring{x}_{e,f_{i}}=0
and

\mathring{x}_{e,f_{j}}=0
, yields the following constraints on
\zeta_{e,f_{i}}

and
\zeta_{e,f_{j}}
:

\displaystyle\zeta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\zeta_{e,f_{j}}

\displaystyle=Z_{e}^{-1}.

(4.5)

•

A possible choice for the remaining function values of
M_{e,f_{i}}
and

M_{e,f_{j}}
is given as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-45.52458ptM_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e})\triangleq\zeta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\chi_{e,f_{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\cdot\begin{cases}-\mu_{e,f_{j}}(\mathring{x}_{e})&x_{e}=0,\ \mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\setminus\{0\}\\
\delta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot[x_{e}\!=\!\mathring{x}_{e}]+\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e})\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(\mathring{x}_{e})&x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}\setminus\{0\},\ \mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\setminus\{0\}\end{cases},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\hskip-45.52458ptM_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e})\triangleq\zeta_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\chi_{e,f_{j}}

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\cdot\begin{cases}-\mu_{e,f_{i}}(\mathring{x}_{e})&x_{e}=0,\ \mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\setminus\{0\}\\
\delta_{e,f_{j}}\cdot[x_{e}\!=\!\mathring{x}_{e}]+\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e})\cdot\mu_{e,f_{i}}(\mathring{x}_{e})&x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}\setminus\{0\},\ \mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\setminus\{0\}\end{cases},

(4.7)

where
\chi_{e,f_{i}}
and
\chi_{e,f_{j}}
are real-valued constants satisfying

\displaystyle\chi_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\chi_{e,f_{j}}

\displaystyle=1,

(4.8)

where
\delta_{e,f_{i}}
,
\delta_{e,f_{j}}
are real-valued constants satisfying

\displaystyle\delta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\delta_{e,f_{j}}

\displaystyle=Z_{e},

(4.9)

where for
\beta_{e}(0)\neq 1
, the constants
\delta_{e,f_{i}}
,
\delta_{e,f_{j}}
,
\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}
, and

\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\delta_{e,f_{i}}+Z_{e}\cdot\bigl(1-\beta_{e}(0)\bigr)\cdot\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{e,f_{j}}(0),

(4.10)

\displaystyle\delta_{e,f_{j}}+Z_{e}\cdot\bigl(1-\beta_{e}(0)\bigr)\cdot\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{e,f_{i}}(0),

(4.11)

where for
\beta_{e}(0)=1
, the constants
\delta_{e,f_{i}}
,
\delta_{e,f_{j}}
satisfy (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), respectively, and the constants
\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}
and

\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}
satisfy

\displaystyle 1+\delta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}+\delta_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}

\displaystyle=0,

(4.12)

and where
\beta_{e}(0)=\mu_{e,f_{i}}(0)\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(0)/Z_{e}
(see
Definition
12
).
The proof showing that the above gives a valid
choice for
M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{j}}
is given in
Appendix
C.1
.

Let
\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}
and
\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}
to be the matrices associated with
M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{i}}
, respectively,
with rows indices
x_{e}
and column indices
\mathring{x}_{e}
.
If
\mu_{e,f_{i}}(0)=\mu_{e,f_{j}}(0)=1
and
\mu_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e})=\mu_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e})=0
for
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}\setminus\{0\}
, then from (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), the matrices
\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}
and
\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}
become

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}=\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}=\bm{I},

where
\bm{I}
is the identity matrix of size
|\mathcal{X}_{e}|\times|\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}|
.
To sum up, in this case, the LCT is to set “0” in the associated off-diagonal entries in the matrices
\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}
and
\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}
.

Note that when
|\mathcal{X}_{e}|=2
, say
\mathcal{X}_{e}=\{0,1\}
, and,
correspondingly,
\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}=\{0,1\}
, the function values of

M_{e,f_{i}}(1,1)
and
M_{e,f_{j}}(1,1)
are uniquely fixed:

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{i}}(1,1)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\chi_{e,f_{i}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{j}}(0),

\displaystyle M_{e,f_{j}}(1,1)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\chi_{e,f_{j}}\cdot\mu_{e,f_{i}}(0),

i.e., although different choices are possible for
\delta_{e,f_{i}}
,

\delta_{e,f_{j}}
,
\epsilon_{e,f_{i}}
, and
\epsilon_{e,f_{j}}
, they all
lead to the same function values for
M_{e,f_{i}}(1,1)
and

M_{e,f_{j}}(1,1)
.

2.

(generalization of the second step above) Nothing is done in this step.

3.

(generalization of the third step above) For every function node

f\in\mathcal{F}
, we do the following.

•

Let

\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\triangleq(\mathring{x}_{e})_{e\in\partial f}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}
and

\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.

•

Define

\displaystyle\mathring{f}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\bm{x}_{\partial f}}f(\bm{x}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}M_{e,f}(x_{e},\mathring{x}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pt\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}.

(4.13)

•

Finally, the LCT
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
of
\mathsf{N}
based on
\bm{\mu}
is
defined to be the NFG with function node set
\mathcal{F}
, edge set
\mathcal{E}
,
edge-function node incidence as for
\mathsf{N}
, where for every function
node
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the associated local function is
\mathring{f}
,
and where for every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, the associated variable
is
\mathring{x}_{e}
and the associated alphabet is
\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.
With this, the global function of
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
is defined to be the mapping

\displaystyle\mathring{g}:\prod\limits_{e}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{f}\mathring{f}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}).

\triangle

Remark 73
.

The following remarks provide insights into the limitations and characteristics of the LCT in different scenarios.

1.

There are S-NFGs where it is not possible to obtain
M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{j}}
for
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
based on the SPA fixed-point messages.
Consider a single-cycle S-NFG in Item
3
in Remark
21
.
The SPA fixed-point message vector satisfies

Z_{e}=0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
and thus the LCT does not work due to the equations (
4.3
)–(
4.5
). The existence of the LCT for this single-cycle S-NFG would contradict the non-diagonalizability of the matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

2.

The LCT
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
of
\mathsf{N}
is in general
not
an S-NFG. This
is because some of the local functions of
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
might take on
negative real values. With that, also the global function of
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}

might take on negative real values.

3.

In the case of a binary alphabet
\mathcal{X}_{e}
, the LCT we obtain is the same
as the one presented in
[
CC06
]
. However, for non-binary alphabets
\mathcal{X}_{e}
,
our LCT, although inspired by previous works such as
[
CC06
,
CC07
,
Mor15a
]
, is in general different. Notably, our approach does not require any assumption of non-zeroness on the SPA fixed-point messages.

\triangle

Proposition 74
.

Consider an S-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and let

\bm{\mu}
be an SPA
fixed-point message vector for
\mathsf{N}
.
The LCT of
\mathsf{N}
, as specified in
Definition
72
, is denoted by
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
.
(Recall that in Assumption
13
,
we assume
Z_{e}(\bm{\mu})>0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, which ensures that the LCT of
\mathsf{N}
is well-defined.)
The main properties of
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
are listed as follows.

1.

The partition function remains the same:

\displaystyle Z(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z\bigl(\mathring{\mathsf{N}}\bigr).

2.

The SPA-based Bethe partition function of the original S-NFG is equal to the global function value of the all-zero configuration of

\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle=\mathring{g}(\bm{0}).

3.

For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
and every
\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}
with
exactly one non-zero component, i.e.,

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})=1,

it holds that

\displaystyle\mathring{f}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})

\displaystyle=0,

where
w_{\mathrm{H}}(\cdot)
is the Hamming weight of the argument.

4.

For any configuration
\mathring{\bm{x}}\in\prod\limits_{e}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
in
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
,
we define
the associated subgraph
\bigl(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})\bigr)
, which consists of
the set of vertices
\mathcal{F}

and the set of edges

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})=\{e\in\mathcal{E}\ |\ \mathring{x}_{e}\neq 0\}.

The subgraph
\bigl(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})\bigr)
is called a generalized loop
if this subgraph
does not contain any leaves (vertices of degree one). (Note that for the all-zero
configuration
\mathring{\bm{x}}=\bm{0}
, the subgraph
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}}))

is also considered as a generalized loop.)

It holds that if
\mathring{\bm{x}}
is a valid configuration for
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
,
then the subgraph
\bigl(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})\bigr)
is a generalized
loop, or equivalently,
if
\bigl(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})\bigr)
is not a generalized loop
for the configuration
\mathring{\bm{x}}
, then
\mathring{g}(\mathring{\bm{x}})=0
.
If
\mathsf{N}
is cycle-free and with that
also
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
is cycle-free, then

\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
has only one valid configuration, which is
the all-zero configuration and corresponds to a generalized loop.

5.

If
Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
it holds that

\displaystyle Z(\mathsf{N})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})\cdot\left(1+\sum\limits_{\mathring{\bm{x}}}\frac{\mathring{g}(\mathring{\bm{x}})}{\mathring{g}(\bm{0})}\right),

where the summation is over all
\mathring{\bm{x}}\in\prod\limits_{e}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathring{\bm{x}})
corresponds to a non-zero generalized loop of

\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
. The term
\mathring{g}(\mathring{\bm{x}})/\mathring{g}(\bm{0})
can
be considered to be “correction terms” to the Bethe approximation. Note
that if
\mathsf{N}
is cycle-free then there are no correction terms,
implying the well-known result that

Z(\mathsf{N})=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})
.

6.

The SPA fixed-point message vector

\bm{\mu}
for

\mathsf{N}
induces an SPA fixed-point message vector

\mathring{\bm{\mu}}\triangleq(\mathring{\bm{\mu}}_{e,f})_{f\in\partial e,e\in\mathcal{E}}
for

\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{\mu}}_{e,f}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1,&0,&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\!\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}|},\hskip 23.49976pte\in\mathcal{E},\,f\in\partial e.

7.

While the above properties are shared by all variants of the LCT, the
LCT in Definition
72
has the following additional
properties (that are not necessarily shared by the other LCT variants):

(a)

For every
e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
, the functions
M_{e,f_{i}}
and

M_{e,f_{j}}
are formally symmetric in the sense that swapping
f_{i}
and

f_{j}
in the definition of
M_{e,f_{i}}
leads to the definition of

M_{e,f_{j}}
, and vice-versa.

(b)

Assume that
M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{j}}
are defined based on the
choices
\zeta_{e,f_{i}}=\zeta_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq Z_{e}^{-1/2}
,

\chi_{e,f_{i}}=\chi_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq 1
,

\delta_{e,f_{i}}=\delta_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq Z_{e}^{1/2}
. Let

e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
be an edge such that

\mu_{e,f_{i}}(x_{e})=\mu_{e,f_{j}}(x_{e})\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
. Then
the matrices
\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}

and
\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}
are equal
and orthogonal.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.2
.
∎

We conclude this section with some remarks.

•

Property
2
in Proposition
74
states that the LCT is a reparamerization of the global function based on the SPA fixed point message vector
\bm{\mu}
such that
the global function evaluated at
\mathring{\bm{x}}=\bm{0}
equals the
\bm{\mu}
-based Bethe partition function. The LCT can be viewed as shifting the bulk of the sum over the variables of the global function to some known configuration as well as keeping the partition function unchanged.

•

Property
6
in Proposition
74
highlights that after applying the LCT on
\mathsf{N}
based on the SPA fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
, one of the SPA fixed-point message vectors for the LCT-transformed factor graph
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
has a simple structure.
Specifically, this simple SPA fixed-point message vector, denoted by
\mathring{\bm{\mu}}
, satisfies

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathring{\mathsf{N}},\mathring{\bm{\mu}}),

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{e,f_{i}}(\mathring{x}_{e})

\displaystyle=\mathring{\mu}_{e,f_{j}}(\mathring{x}_{e})=[\mathring{x}_{e}\!=\!0],\hskip 23.49976pt\mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e},\,e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}.

This simple structure partially motivates the proof of the graph-cover theorem in Section
6
.

4.2
LCT for DE-NFGs

The LCT for DE-NFGs has many similarities to the LCT for S-NFGs. Therefore, we will mostly highlight the differences in this section.

Definition 75
.

The LCT for DE-NFGs is a natural extension of the LCT for S-NFG in Definition
72
.
Further definitions are made for DE-NFGs.
For each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we apply the following changes.

•

The alphabet
\mathcal{X}_{e}
is replaced by the alphabet
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}=\mathcal{X}_{e}\times\mathcal{X}_{e}
.

•

The special element
0\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
is replaced by the special element

\tilde{0}\triangleq(0,0)\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.

•

The variable
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
is replaced by the variable

\tilde{x}_{e}=(x_{e},x^{\prime}_{e})\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.

•

The alphabet

\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}\triangleq\mathcal{X}_{e}
is
replaced by the alphabet

\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{e}\triangleq\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
.

•

The element
0\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
is replaced by the element

\tilde{0}\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{e}
.

•

The variable
\mathring{x}_{e}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}
is replaced by the variable

\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e}=(\mathring{x}_{e},\mathring{x}^{\prime}_{e})\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{e}
.

Note that in the definition for DE-NFG, we consider that the constants
\zeta_{e,f_{j}}
,
\chi_{e,f_{j}}
,
\delta_{e,f}
, and
\epsilon_{e,f}
are real-valued.
For any finite set
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{e}=\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}},

where

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq\prod\limits_{e\in\mathcal{I}}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{e}.

The associated collection of variables is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime})\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{\mathcal{I}}.

For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathring{f}(\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\partial f})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum\limits_{\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}}f(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})\cdot\prod\limits_{e\in\partial f}M_{e,f}(\tilde{x}_{e},\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e}),\hskip 23.49976pt\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\partial f}\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{\partial f}.

(4.14)

\triangle

If there is no ambiguity, for any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
,
we will use
\sum\limits_{\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
instead of

\sum\limits_{\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
for simplicity.

Proposition 76
.

Consider a DE-NFG
\mathsf{N}
and an SPA
fixed-point message vector
\bm{\mu}
for
\mathsf{N}
.
Let
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
denote the LCT of
\mathsf{N}
obtained by
\bm{\mu}
, as specified in
Definition
75
. The main properties of
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
are listed as follows.

1.

Natural extension of Property
1
in Proposition
74
:

Z(\mathsf{N})=Z\bigl(\mathring{\mathsf{N}}\bigr).

2.

Natural extension of Property
2
in Proposition
74
:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{B,SPA}}(\mathsf{N},\bm{\mu})=\mathring{g}(\tilde{\bm{0}})=\prod\limits_{f}\mathring{f}(\tilde{\bm{0}}),

where
\tilde{\bm{0}}=(\tilde{0},\ldots,\tilde{0})
.

3.

Natural extension of Property
3
in Proposition
74
: for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
and every
\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}\in\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{\partial f}
with exactly one non-zero component,
i.e.,

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})=1,

with
w_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f})\triangleq\sum\limits_{e\in\partial f}\bigl[\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e}\!\neq\!\tilde{0}\bigr]
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathring{f}(\mathring{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{\partial f})

\displaystyle=0.

4.

Natural extension of Property
4
in Proposition
74
.

5.

Natural extension of Property
5
in Proposition
74
.

6.

Natural extension of Property
6
in Proposition
74

Nevertheless, because of its importance
for later parts of this thesis, the statement is included here. Namely, the
SPA fixed-point message vector

\bm{\mu}
for

\mathsf{N}
induces an SPA fixed-point message vector
\mathring{\bm{\mu}}=(\mathring{\bm{\mu}}_{e,f})_{e\in\partial f,f\in\mathcal{F}}

for

\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
with

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{e,f}(\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e})

\displaystyle=\left[\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e}\!=\!\tilde{0}\right],\hskip 23.49976pt\mathring{\tilde{x}}_{e}\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{e}.

7.

Natural extension of Property
7
in Proposition
74
.
Note that Property
7b
is slightly different:
Assume that
M_{e,f_{i}}
and
M_{e,f_{j}}
are defined based on the
choices
\zeta_{e,f_{i}}=\zeta_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq Z_{e}^{-1/2}
,

\chi_{e,f_{i}}=\chi_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq 1
,

\delta_{e,f_{i}}=\delta_{e,f_{j}}\triangleq Z_{e}^{1/2}
. Let

e=(f_{i},f_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
be an edge such that

\mu_{e,f_{i}}(\tilde{x}_{e})=\overline{\mu_{e,f_{j}}(\tilde{x}_{e})}
for all
x_{e}\in\mathcal{X}_{e}
. In this case, both
\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}
and
\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}
are unitary matrices with row indices
x_{e}
and column indices
\mathring{x}_{e}
, and they satisfy

\bm{M}_{e,f_{i}}=\bm{M}_{e,f_{j}}^{\mathsf{H}}
.

8.

For each
e=(f_{i},f_{j})
, the Choi-matrix representations

\bm{C}_{M_{e,f_{i}}}
and
\bm{C}_{M_{e,f_{i}}}

are complex-valued Hermitian matrices.

9.

For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
the Choi-matrix representation
\bm{C}_{\mathring{f}}
is a complex-valued Hermitian matrix,
where
\bm{C}_{\mathring{f}}
is defined to be a matrix with row indices
\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}
and column indices
\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{\mathring{f}}\triangleq\Bigl(\mathring{f}(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f},\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}^{\prime})\Bigr)_{(\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f},\mathring{\bm{x}}_{\partial f}^{\prime})\in\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{\partial f}}.

10.

The resulting DE-NFG
\mathring{\mathsf{N}}
is again a weak-sense DE-NFG.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.3
.
∎

Most of the LCT properties for S-NFG can be straightforwardly extended to the case of DE-NFG. We note that applying the LCT to a strict-sense DE-NFG or a weak-sense DE-NFG yields in another weak-sense DE-NFG, which is similar to the property of the LCT for S-NFG as discussed in Item
2
in Remark
73
.

Chapter 5
Symmetric Subspace Transform (SST)
